\end{equation}
at $\Delta\chi^2=0.2$ from Planck's best fit: the baryon ratio passes. I5's $5/2$ lies $4.6\sigma$ away (its budget, $\omega_b=0.02275$,

(\S25.11).

present at the resonance (H3, \S36.7), $\Omega_\Lambda({\rm now})=27/4\pi^2$. Flatness, the derived baryon ratio and the neutrino masses of
\S42.4, counted inside $\Omega_m$ as everywhere in the budget, fix $\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_c=2.580$ ($2.568$ without the neutrinos), and the

\section{The Arrow of Time: Three of Space, One of Time}\label{sec:arrow}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{26.1\quad The $3{:}1$ ratio}
Space has three dimensions and time has one. In an expanding universe a volume grows as $a^3$ and a length as $a$:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\ln V}{dt}=3H,\qquad \frac{d\ln a}{dt}=H .
\end{equation}
Space, measured by volume, expands at rate $3$ while the ruler expands at rate $1$.

\subsection*{26.2\quad Two frames}
\sD\ The two frames are cosmic time $t$, kept by matter, and conformal time $\eta=\int dt/a$, in which the seed $h=r$ is exact
(\S1.10); light covers the comoving distance $c\eta$. For matter, $\rho\propto a^{-3}$ and $H^2\propto\rho$ give $a\propto t^{2/3}$ and
\begin{equation}
\eta_0=\int_0^{t_0}\frac{dt}{a}=t_0^{2/3}\int_0^{t_0}t^{-2/3}\,dt=3\,t_0 .
\end{equation}
In $d$ spatial dimensions $a\propto t^{2/d}$ and $\eta_0=\tfrac{d}{d-2}\,t_0$: the $3$ is the dimension of space, and the space frame runs
three times the matter frame.

\subsection*{26.3\quad Ages}
For pure matter $t_0=2/3H_0$ and $\eta_0=3t_0$ exactly. With the full contents---radiation shortens the conformal age, the vacuum
lengthens both---the budget gives $t_0=13.79$ Gyr and $c\eta_0=46.1$ Gly $=3.18\,c/H_0$, the radius of the observable universe, so
$\eta_0=3.34\,t_0$. Both ages are correct; they belong to different frames.

\subsection*{26.4\quad Acceleration}
\sS\ Gravity is sourced by the active mass density, counting the one time component and the three space components of the stress:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\ddot a}{a}=-\frac{4\pi G}{3}\Big(\rho+\frac{3p}{c^2}\Big)
=-\frac{4\pi G}{3}\big(\underbrace{\rho}_{\text{1 of time}}
+\underbrace{3\,p/c^2}_{\text{3 of space}}\big).
\end{equation}
With $\weff\equiv p_{\rm tot}/\rho_{\rm tot}c^2$, a flat universe has
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{1+3\weff}{2},\qquad 1+q=\frac32\,(1+\weff),
\label{eq:qweff}
\end{equation}
so tensions in the three spatial directions that outweigh the one temporal energy accelerate the expansion. For matter plus vacuum
$\weff=-\Omega_\Lambda$ and
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{\Omega_m}{2}-\Omega_\Lambda=\frac{1-3\,\Omega_\Lambda}{2}:
\label{eq:q}
\end{equation}
the universe accelerates exactly when $3\,\Omega_\Lambda>1$, three of space outweighing one of time. Today $q_0=-0.526$ (Planck: $-0.527$).

The tension comes from the defect: one extended in $n$ spatial dimensions has tension $-\rho c^2$ along each and none across, so averaged
over orientations \cite{Vilenkin1981,SpergelPen1997}
\begin{equation}
p=-\frac n3\,\rho c^2,\qquad w=-\frac n3,\qquad \rho+\frac{3p}{c^2}=(1-n)\,\rho .
\label{eq:wn}
\end{equation}
Strings ($n=1$) coast, walls have $w=-\tfrac23$, and a defect filling space has $w=-1$ and active mass $-2\rho$: the horizon defect spread
through space is the vacuum, one of time minus three of space.

\subsection*{26.5\quad Matter creates time: the maximum-power epoch}
Matter's stress has one entry, the time--time energy; the vacuum's has four equal entries, one of time and three of space. The time-like
share of all stress in a flat matter-plus-vacuum universe is therefore
\begin{equation}
\tau=\frac{\Omega_m+\Omega_\Lambda}{\Omega_m+4\,\Omega_\Lambda}=\frac{1}{1+3\,\Omega_\Lambda}.
\end{equation}
\sH\ (H1) Matter is the time-carrying content of the universe, and the present lies near the moment when matter's share of the budget
equals the time-like share of the stress, $\Omega_m(z)=\tau(z)$. With $\Omega_\Lambda=1-\Omega_m$,
\begin{equation}
3\,\Omega_m^2-4\,\Omega_m+1=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(3\,\Omega_m-1)(\Omega_m-1)=0 ,
\end{equation}
whose non-trivial root is $\Omega_m=\tfrac13$ (in $d$ spatial dimensions, $1/d$). The condition has a dynamical meaning:

\begin{theorem}[Maximum-power epoch]\label{thm:maxpower}
\sD\ For a flat universe of matter and vacuum the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep0pt
\item[(a)] $\Omega_m=\tau$: matter's share equals the time-like share of the stress;
\item[(b)] $\Omega_m=\tfrac13$;
\item[(c)] $q=-\tfrac12$;
\item[(d)] $\dot r=c/2$: the Hubble radius recedes at half the speed of light;
\item[(e)] $\weff=-\tfrac23$: the universe as a whole has the equation of state of a
domain-wall network, $n=2$ in~\eqref{eq:wn};
\item[(f)] $\dot\EH=\Pmax=c^5/4G$: the horizon energy grows at exactly the maximum power
of the axiom.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a)$\iff$(b) is shown above. With $\Omega_\Lambda=1-\Omega_m$,
equation~\eqref{eq:q} reads $q=(3\Omega_m-2)/2$, so (b)$\iff$(c). The master rate gives
$\dot r=c(1+q)$ (equation~\eqref{eq:master}), so (c)$\iff$(d). Equation~\eqref{eq:qweff}
gives (c)$\iff$(e). Finally $\dot\EH=2(1+q)\Pmax$ (equation~\eqref{eq:power}), so
(c)$\iff$(f).
\end{proof}
Matter's carrying time is therefore not a counting rule: it marks the horizon's growth passing through the axiom's maximum power, from
$3\Pmax$ in pure matter toward zero in de Sitter. $\Pmax$ bounds the luminosity of any localized source (\S43B.6); the horizon's growth is
not a luminosity, so it may exceed $\Pmax$ early, and the crossing is a distinguished event, not a violation. With the budget it lies at
$z=0.027$, $0.38$ Gyr ago ($z=0.028$ with Planck's $\Omega_m$), and today $\dot\EH/\Pmax=2(1+q_0)=0.948$: we live just after the
maximum-power epoch. The cosmic coincidence problem becomes a condition, and the condition a statement about the axiom.

\subsection*{26.6\quad The arrow}
\sD\ The cell count scales as $N\propto r^2\propto H^{-2}$. For a flat universe the Friedmann and continuity equations give
$\dot H=-4\pi G(\rho+p/c^2)$, so
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN}{dt}=-2N\frac{\dot H}{H}=\frac{8\pi GN}{H}\Big(\rho+\frac{p}{c^2}\Big)\ \ge\ 0
\label{eq:arrow}
\end{equation}
whenever $\rho+p/c^2\ge0$. The horizon's cell count, and with it the information $N/2$, never decreases; it stays constant only for pure
vacuum. This is the arrow of time in GRUT and the generalized second law of the Hubble horizon \cite{Davies1987}.

\subsection*{26.7\quad Consequences of the budget}
\sP\ From $\Omega_m=0.3160$, $\Omega_\Lambda=0.6839$ (\S25.11) and the flat Friedmann equation:
\begin{gather}
q_0=-0.526,\qquad
z_{\rm acc}=\Big(\frac{2\Omega_\Lambda}{\Omega_m}\Big)^{1/3}-1=0.630,\\
z_{m\Lambda}=\Big(\frac{\Omega_\Lambda}{\Omega_m}\Big)^{1/3}-1=0.293,\qquad
z_{3:1}=\Big(\frac{\Omega_\Lambda}{2\Omega_m}\Big)^{1/3}-1=0.027,
\end{gather}
and $H_0t_0=\frac{2}{3\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda}}\,\mathrm{arcsinh}\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_m}=0.9501$.

\emph{The Hubble constant.} The CMB measures the acoustic angle $\theta_*$ to $0.03\%$ \cite{Planck2018}. With $\Omega_\Lambda=27/4\pi^2$,
the baryon ratio and $\sum m_\nu=59.34$ meV (\S42.4) fixed, CAMB \cite{Lewis2000} is solved for the $H_0$ that reproduces Planck's measured
$100\theta_*=1.04110\pm0.00031$:
\begin{gather}
H_0=67.35\pm0.06\,(\theta_*)\ \mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}},
\label{eq:H0pred}\\
\omega_b=0.02248\ (+0.7\sigma),\qquad \omega_c=0.12022\ (+0.2\sigma),\nonumber
\end{gather}
with $r_{\rm drag}=146.9$ Mpc and $t_0=13.79$ Gyr; $a_0=(\sqrt3/\pi^2)\,cH_0=1.148\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ lies inside the
measured band (\S22.5). $H_0$ coincides with Planck's $\Lambda$CDM value and $5.5\sigma$ below SH0ES ($4.8\sigma$ with the dilated
scatter of the local rate): GRUT predicts that the tension resolves at the low value, every distance ladder calibrated alike converging
on $67.3$ (\S53.9). The $5/2$ budget's $\omega_b=0.02275$ ($+2.5\sigma$) was its material variance, set by the dark ratio
(Appendix~\ref{app:variance}).

\emph{Curvature.} The budget is flat; a spatial $S^3$ (\S4.6) must have radius $R>18\,r$.

\subsection*{26.8\quad The defect ladder of cosmic history}
\begin{theorem}[Defect ladder]\label{thm:ladder}
\sD\ In any flat Friedmann universe the horizon energy grows at
\begin{equation}
\dot\EH=3\,(1+\weff)\,\Pmax .
\end{equation}
When the effective equation of state equals the value of an $n$-dimensional defect,
$\weff=-n/3$, the horizon grows at $\dot\EH=(3-n)\,\Pmax$ and recedes at
$\dot r=\tfrac12(3-n)\,c$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Combine $\dot\EH=2(1+q)\Pmax$ and $\dot r=c(1+q)$ with $1+q=\tfrac32(1+\weff)$,
equation~\eqref{eq:qweff}.
\end{proof}
Radiation, $w=+\tfrac13$, is $n=-1$ of the same formula. Cosmic history is the ladder $n=-1,0,1,2,3$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccl@{}}
\toprule
stage & $n$ & $\weff$ & $q$ & $\dot\EH/\Pmax$ & $\dot r/c$ & epoch (budget) \\
\midrule
radiation & $-1$ & $+\tfrac13$ & $1$ & $4$ & $2$ & early universe \\
matter & $0$ & $0$ & $\tfrac12$ & $3$ & $\tfrac32$ & $z\gg1$ \\
strings: acceleration begins & $1$ & $-\tfrac13$ & $0$ & $2$ & $1$ & $z=0.630$, $6.1$ Gyr ago \\
walls: maximum-power epoch & $2$ & $-\tfrac23$ & $-\tfrac12$ & $1$ & $\tfrac12$ & $z=0.027$, $0.38$ Gyr ago \\
vacuum: de Sitter & $3$ & $-1$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $0$ & future \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For matter plus vacuum the crossings sit at $\Omega_\Lambda(z)=n/3$, that is $(1+z_n)^3=(\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_m)(3-n)/n$: the dark-energy era
is divided into thirds, one per spatial dimension the defect fills, and the present lies between the second and third rungs
(Figure~\ref{fig:ladder}).

% =====================================================================
\section{The Light Cone and the Speed of Light}\label{sec:lightcone}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{27.1\quad $c$ given}
$c=299\,792\,458\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, exact.

\subsection*{27.2\quad Cone as light cone}
The seed is the light cone~\eqref{eq:null}; the causal structure of spacetime is that
cone placed at every event.

\subsection*{27.3\quad Terminal state}
Massless quanta move at $c$ at every epoch, $v^2=c^2$. Massive bodies lose peculiar
momentum as the universe expands, $p\propto1/a$, and settle into the comoving frame.
If the dark-sector length $L_*$ grows with the horizon, as in the cosmic reset, the
mass $m_\delta=\sqrt8\,\hbar/cL_*$ falls to zero and that sector joins the massless
one. The terminal state is then the light-cone state, with every remaining degree of
freedom on the null cone.

% =====================================================================
\section{The Lorentz Structure}\label{sec:lorentz}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{28.1\quad From the cone}
\sS\ Place the seed cone at every event. The Alexandrov--Zeeman theorem
\cite{Zeeman1964}: in dimension three or more, every bijection of spacetime that
preserves the order defined by the cones (``$y$ lies inside the future cone of $x$''),
in both directions, is a Lorentz transformation composed with a translation and a
dilation. No continuity or linearity is assumed. The Lorentz group follows from the
cone alone.

The dilation is fixed by the anchors of \S0.2: the cone does not choose a length, the
anchor $r$ does. The count $3+1$ comes from stability. In $d\ge4$ spatial dimensions no
bound orbit is stable, and in $d\le2$ the potential does not decay, so $d=3$ is the
unique dimension with both stable orbits and free separation (Ehrenfest
\cite{Ehrenfest1917}).

\subsection*{28.2\quad Invariant interval}
\begin{equation}
ds^2=-c^2dt^2+dx^2+dy^2+dz^2 ,
\end{equation}
with the seed as $ds^2=0$. For the closed universe of \S4.6,
$ds^2=-c^2dt^2+a^2(t)R^2[d\chi^2+\sin^2\chi(d\theta^2+\sin^2\theta\,d\phi^2)]$.

% =====================================================================
\section{Newtonian Gravity}\label{sec:newton}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{29.1\quad Inverse square}
\sS\ The field of a point mass carries the same flux through every sphere around it
(Gauss). In $d$ spatial dimensions the sphere's area grows as $r^{d-1}$, so
$g\propto r^{-(d-1)}$, and in three dimensions
\begin{equation}
F=\frac{GMm}{r^2},\qquad g=\frac{GM}{r^2}.
\end{equation}
The $1/r$ potential is one of only two central potentials whose bound orbits all close
(Bertrand), so Kepler's ellipses are a consequence of $d=3$.

\subsection*{29.2\quad $G$ from the axiom}
$G=c^4/F$ (\S12).

\subsection*{29.3\quad Equivalence principle}
Gravity is carried by a massless spin-2 field on the light cone. Weinberg
\cite{Weinberg1964} proved that Lorentz invariance of the amplitude for emitting such
a quantum forces every particle to couple to it with the same strength, so inertial
and gravitational mass are equal. MICROSCOPE confirms this to $|\eta|\lesssim10^{-15}$
\cite{Touboul2022}.

% =====================================================================
\section{General Relativity}\label{sec:GR}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{30.1\quad Curvature from the defect}
\sS\ Curvature is what the deficit measures (\S1.7). Lovelock's theorem
\cite{Lovelock1971}: in four dimensions the only symmetric, divergence-free tensor
built from the metric and its first two derivatives is $aG_{\mu\nu}+bg_{\mu\nu}$. The
second-order field equations with local conservation are therefore
\begin{equation}
G_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^4}\,T_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi}{F}\,T_{\mu\nu},
\end{equation}
the last form using the axiom: geometry couples to matter with strength $8\pi$ divided
by the universe force, or $2\pi$ divided by the one-cone force. Jacobson \cite{Jacobson1995} obtains the same equation from the
horizon relation $\delta Q=T\,dS$ with $S=A/4\lP^2$, the entropy of \S20.

\subsection*{30.2\quad $\Lambda$ from the vacuum}
$\Lambda=8\pi G\rho_\Lambda/c^2=3\Omega_\Lambda H_0^2/c^2=3\Omega_\Lambda/r^2$, which is
$1.09\times10^{-52}\ \mathrm{m^{-2}}$ with Planck values.

\subsection*{30.3\quad Schwarzschild and the horizon}
$r_s=2GM/c^2=2c^2M/F$, and for the horizon mass $r_s=r$~\eqref{eq:horizon}. The
defect form of mass~\eqref{eq:defectmass} is exact for straight defects in $3+1$
dimensions, $\delta=8\pi G\mu/c^2$, and for point masses in $2+1$ dimensions,
$\delta=8\pi G_3M/c^2$ \cite{DJtH1984}.

% =====================================================================
\section{Electromagnetism}\label{sec:EM}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{31.1\quad The electric $U(1)$}
\sD\ The three generators carry the phases of $\mathbb Z_3\subset U(1)$, and the fermion
generations of \S42 carry the generators. Letting the phase vary continuously from point to point,
$\psi\to e^{iq\Lambda(x)/\hbar}\psi$, requires a connection,
$D_\mu=\partial_\mu-\tfrac{iq}{\hbar}A_\mu$ with $A_\mu\to A_\mu+\partial_\mu\Lambda$, so that
$D_\mu\psi\to e^{iq\Lambda/\hbar}D_\mu\psi$. This $U(1)$ acts on the fermions, not on the defect
condensate. Were the condensate charged, the Higgs mechanism would give the photon the mass
$m_\gamma=\sqrt2\,q_\delta e f_\delta\phi_0$ (\S41.7), and the bound $m_\gamma<10^{-18}$ eV
\cite{PDG2024} would require $|q_\delta|<4\times10^{-46}$. Charges come in units of $e/3$ (\S31.4),
and the smallest of them would give $m_\gamma\sim10^{27}$ eV: \sD\ the condensate is exactly
neutral and the photon exactly massless.

\subsection*{31.2\quad Maxwell from the phase gradient}
The gauge-invariant field strength is $F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_\mu A_\nu-\partial_\nu A_\mu$. The
lowest-order Lorentz- and gauge-invariant Lagrangian,
$-\tfrac{1}{4\mu_0}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-J^\mu A_\mu$, gives Maxwell's equations,
\begin{equation}
\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0J^\nu,\qquad \partial_{[\lambda}F_{\mu\nu]}=0 .
\end{equation}
\sS\ The homogeneous pair is the Bianchi identity of $F=dA$, and the $U(1)$ symmetry conserves the
current, $\partial_\mu J^\mu=0$, as $\partial_\nu\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=0$ requires. Gauge
invariance, $A_\mu\to A_\mu+\partial_\mu\Lambda$, is the continuous closure of the cycle's phase.

\subsection*{31.3\quad Coulomb's law and the electric axiom}
\sD\ In the three spatial dimensions of \S26, Gauss's law $\oint\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf A=q/\varepsilon_0$
gives the field of a point charge, $E=q/4\pi\varepsilon_0d^2$, so two elementary charges repel with
\begin{equation}
F_e=\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0d^2}=\frac{\alpha\hbar c}{d^2},\qquad \alpha\equiv\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0\hbar c} .
\end{equation}
This is the electric axiom of \S8.3: the inverse square is the three-dimensionality of space, and
the coupling is $\alpha$. In $D$ spatial dimensions the force would fall as $d^{1-D}$.

\subsection*{31.4\quad Charge quantization}
\sD\ The $C_3$ cycle fixes the number of colours, $N_c=3$ (\S15.3, \sI), and \S42.2 supplies
each generation as weak doublets and singlets with one Higgs doublet. Requiring the $U(1)$ to be
free of gauge anomalies, and the fermion masses to be gauge invariant, fixes every charge. With the
left-handed fields $Q\,(N_c,2)$, $u^c,d^c\,(\bar N_c,1)$, $L\,(1,2)$ and $e^c\,(1,1)$, the mass terms
$QHu^c$, $QH^\dagger d^c$ and $LH^\dagger e^c$ give
$Y_u=-Y_Q-Y_H$, $Y_d=-Y_Q+Y_H$ and $Y_e=-Y_L+Y_H$, so the $[SU(N_c)]^2U(1)$ condition holds
identically; the $[SU(2)]^2U(1)$ condition gives $Y_L=-N_cY_Q$; and the gravitational and cubic
conditions reduce to
\begin{equation}
Y_H-N_cY_Q=0,\qquad -(N_cY_Q-Y_H)^3=0 ,
\end{equation}
both solved by $Y_Q=Y_H/N_c$. With $Q=T_3+Y$ and the Higgs normalized to $Y_H=\tfrac12$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{Q_u=\tfrac12\Big(1+\frac1{N_c}\Big)=\tfrac23,\qquad Q_d=-\tfrac12\Big(1-\frac1{N_c}\Big)=-\tfrac13,\qquad
Q_e=-1,\qquad Q_\nu=0 .}
\label{eq:charges}
\end{equation}
The proton, $uud$, has charge $2Q_u+Q_d=1$, and \sP\ hydrogen is exactly neutral, $q_p+q_e=0$,
against the bound $|q_p+q_e|<10^{-21}e$ \cite{PDG2024}. The thirds of \S15.3 are therefore derived,
not only matched to triality: the colour singlets carry integer charge because the $\mathbb Z_3$
phases close.

\subsection*{31.5\quad The running of $\alpha$}
\sD\ Charged fermions screen the charge. With the charges~\eqref{eq:charges} and the lepton masses,
$m_\tau$ from \S42.3, the one-loop leptonic shift of $\alpha$ to the $Z$ mass is
\begin{equation}
\Delta\alpha_{\rm lep}=\frac{\alpha}{3\pi}\sum_{\ell=e,\mu,\tau}\Big[\ln\frac{M_Z^2}{m_\ell^2}-\frac53\Big]=0.03142,
\end{equation}
to which higher orders add $0.25\%$. The quark contribution below a few GeV is non-perturbative
and is taken from $e^+e^-$ data, $\Delta\alpha_{\rm had}\simeq0.028$ \cite{PDG2024}; together they
give $1/\alpha(M_Z)\simeq128.9$ in the on-shell scheme. The running from $1/137.036$ follows once
the zero-momentum value is given, so a geometric value of $\alpha$ must be that Thomson-limit
coupling.

\subsection*{31.6\quad The value of $\alpha$}
The coupling is $\alpha=F_e/F$ (\S8.3, \S15), calibrated at $1/137.035\,999\,177$. Its geometric
conjecture and exact target, $K=0.041741(3)$, are \S43A/C and \S43F.

% =====================================================================
\section{Quantum Mechanics}\label{sec:QM}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{32.1\quad Wavefunction from cone modes}
The modes of the cycle, $f_n=e^{2\pi imn/3}$, are the discrete prototype: complex
amplitudes whose phases wind around the cone. The wavefunction is their continuum
version, a complex amplitude on configuration space.

\subsection*{32.2\quad $\hbar$}
$\hbar=2\pi Fr^2/Nc$ (\S13).

\subsection*{32.3\quad Born rule}
$P=|\langle a|\psi\rangle|^2$, by Gleason's theorem in dimension $\ge3$ (\S6.4).

\subsection*{32.4\quad Commutator}
\sS\ Space is homogeneous, so translations $T(a)\psi(x)=\psi(x-a)$ form a continuous
unitary group. Stone's theorem \cite{Stone1932} gives $T(a)=e^{-ia\hat p/\hbar}$.
Differentiating at $a=0$ gives $-\psi'=-\tfrac i\hbar\hat p\psi$, so
$\hat p=-i\hbar\,\partial_x$ and
\begin{equation}
[\hat x,\hat p]\psi=x(-i\hbar\psi')+i\hbar(\psi+x\psi')=i\hbar\,\psi .
\end{equation}

% =====================================================================
\section{The Schr\"odinger Equation}\label{sec:schrodinger}
% =====================================================================
\sS\ Time is homogeneous, so time translations form a continuous unitary group,
$U(t)=e^{-i\hat Ht/\hbar}$ (Stone), and
\begin{equation}
i\hbar\,\partial_t\psi=\hat H\psi .
\end{equation}
The kinetic term comes from the mass hyperboloid inside the cone (\S19). With
$\hat p_\mu=-i\hbar\partial_\mu$ the relation $p^\mu p_\mu=-m^2c^2$ becomes the
Klein--Gordon equation $(\Box-m^2c^2/\hbar^2)\phi=0$. Writing
$\phi=\psi\,e^{-imc^2t/\hbar}$, the rest-energy terms cancel, and for slow motion
($|\partial_t^2\psi|\ll(mc^2/\hbar)|\partial_t\psi|$)
\begin{equation}
i\hbar\,\partial_t\psi=-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\nabla^2\psi+V\psi ,
\end{equation}
in agreement with the expansion~\eqref{eq:dispersion}. The potential $V$ is the
interaction of the field in question: gravity through $m\Phi$ (\S37), electromagnetism
through $qA_0$.

% =====================================================================
\section{Thermodynamics}\label{sec:thermo}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{34.1\quad Entropy}
$S=A/4\lP^2=\pi r^2/\lP^2=N/2$ (in units of $k_B$), equal to the information $I$ of
\S20.

\subsection*{34.2\quad Temperature}
A horizon with surface gravity $\kappa$ (an acceleration) has temperature
$T=\hbar\kappa/2\pi ck_B$. For the cosmic horizon $\kappa=cH=c^2/r$, so
\begin{equation}
\TGH=\frac{\hbar H_0}{2\pi k_B}=2.878\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm K\ (73.04),\quad
2.654\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm K\ (67.36).
\end{equation}

\subsection*{34.3\quad Second law}
$dN/dt\ge0$ whenever $\rho+p/c^2\ge0$, equation~\eqref{eq:arrow}: the horizon moves to
ever larger counts.

\subsection*{34.4\quad First law}
The horizon energy is exactly temperature times entropy,
$\EH=k_B\TGH S$~\eqref{eq:ETS}. Its differential form on the apparent horizon, with the
matter energy flowing across it, reproduces the Friedmann equations \cite{CaiKim2005}.

\subsection*{34.5\quad Potentials, heat capacity and the Bekenstein bound}
With $S\propto H^{-2}$ and $T\propto H$, the entropy is $S\propto T^{-2}$, so
\begin{equation}
C=T\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}=-2S .
\end{equation}
The heat capacity is negative, as for any self-gravitating system. Using
$\EH=k_B\TGH S$ (\S24) and $PV=-\rho_\Lambda c^2\cdot\tfrac43\pi r^3=-\EH$ for $w=-1$ in the de Sitter limit
$\rho_\Lambda=\rho_c$:
\begin{equation}
F_{\rm Helm}=\EH-k_B\TGH S=0,\qquad G_{\rm Gibbs}=\EH-k_B\TGH S+PV=-\EH .
\end{equation}
The horizon has zero Helmholtz free energy. The Bekenstein bound is saturated exactly:
\begin{equation}
\frac{2\pi k_B r\EH}{\hbar c}=\frac{2\pi k_B}{\hbar c}\cdot\frac{c}{H_0}\cdot\frac{c^5}{2GH_0}
=\frac{\pi k_Bc^5}{\hbar GH_0^2}=S .
\end{equation}
Read as a spin, the bound is $S\le2\pi L_{\max}/\hbar$, with $L_{\max}=ER/c$ the largest angular momentum an energy $E$ inside
$R$ can carry, reached only when it moves at light speed on the rim; the horizon saturates it because the rim of its turn
moves at $c$ (\S36.11).
The Euler relation does not close on its own: $k_B\TGH S-PV=2\EH\neq\EH$. It closes with
a term conjugate to the deficit,
\begin{equation}
\EH=k_B\TGH S-PV+\mu\,\dtot,\qquad \mu=-\frac{\EH}{\pi}=-\frac{c^5}{2\pi GH_0},
\end{equation}
so the deficit behaves as a conserved charge with its own chemical potential. All four
statements use the sphere quantities; each cone carries a quarter of $\EH$ and half the
area, and the same relations hold with those factors.

% =====================================================================
\section{Statistical Mechanics}\label{sec:statmech}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{35.1, 35.3\quad Partition function and Boltzmann distribution}
\sS\ Maximizing $S=-\sum p_i\ln p_i$ at fixed normalization and fixed mean energy $U$, with
multipliers $\lambda_0$ and $\beta$, gives $-\ln p_i-1-\lambda_0-\beta E_i=0$, so
\begin{equation}
p_i=\frac{e^{-\beta E_i}}{Z},\qquad Z=\sum_i e^{-\beta E_i}
\end{equation}
\cite{Jaynes1957}.

\subsection*{35.2\quad Ensembles}
The horizon has $\Omega=e^{S}=e^{N/2}$ microstates. Each bit occupies
$4\ln2\,\lP^2=2.77\,\lP^2$ of horizon area, and each of the $N$ effective cells
carries half a nat. The number of distinct horizon configurations is
$e^{N/2}=2^{N/2\ln2}$.

\subsection*{35.4\quad Ergodicity}
The cycle $C_3$ visits its three states in turn, so time averages equal the uniform
average over the cycle.

% =====================================================================
\section{Cosmology}\label{sec:cosmo}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{36.1\quad $H_0$}
$H_0=c/r$.

\subsection*{36.2--36.3\quad Density}
\begin{equation}
\rho=\frac{\MH}{V_s}=\frac{Fr/2c^2}{\tfrac43\pi r^3}=\frac{3F}{8\pi c^2r^2}
=\frac{3H_0^2}{8\pi G}=\rho_c .
\end{equation}

\subsection*{36.4\quad Flatness}
$\Omega_{\rm tot}=1$ is the horizon condition~\eqref{eq:horizon}. With the $S^3$ of
\S4.6 as the spatial section, $\Omega_{\rm tot}=1+(r/R)^2$.

\subsection*{36.5\quad Scale bridge}
$a_0=(\sqrt3/\pi^2)\,cH_0$, with $\alpha_{\rm TRSD}=\sqrt3/\pi^2=0.175493$.

% =====================================================================
\subsection*{36.6\quad The selection of the present epoch}
\sM\ With the budget, today is $t_0=13.82$ Gyr and the maximum-power crossing ($\Omega_m=\tfrac13$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:maxpower}) was at $13.56$ Gyr: $|\ln(t_0/t_\times)|=0.019$. For an observer
placed at random in log time over two decades of cosmic history, the chance of lying this close
to the crossing is $0.8\%$. \sH\ A principle of extremal horizon power would place the observer
at $\Omega_m=\tfrac13$ exactly; the resonance budget puts today $0.38$ Gyr past it, at $\Omega_m=0.316$.
The coincidence is quantified; no principle that selects the epoch is derived, and the
hypothesis stays.

\subsection*{36.7\quad Now: where the horizon meets the final rate}
\sH\ The internal rotation is held at a constant rate (\S23), and the one constant rate of the late universe is the final
expansion rate $H_\infty$, set by the final horizon; the rotation turns at $H_\infty$. \sD\ The horizon's lowest $C_3$ mode
has frequency $(3\sqrt3/2\pi)H(t)$, which falls as the universe expands, and it meets the rotation's rate once:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{3\sqrt3}{2\pi}\,H_{\rm now}=H_\infty,\qquad \Omega_\Lambda({\rm now})=\Big(\frac{H_\infty}{H_{\rm now}}\Big)^2=\frac{27}{4\pi^2}=0.6839 .}
\label{eq:now}
\end{equation}
This is the present: the moment the horizon's own mode comes into resonance with the rate of the universe's final state,
the future's rate meeting the present horizon. It is the same statement as the rotation's turning at $0.827H_0$ today
(\S23). It fixes when now is, $t_{\rm now}=0.786/H_\infty=0.950/H_{\rm now}$, and with the derived baryon ratio it gives
$\Omega_m=0.3160$ and $\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_c=2.580$, with the neutrinos of \S42.4 counted inside $\Omega_m$ as everywhere in the budget
($2.568$ without them); the full budget is \S25.11.
Planck's $\Lambda$CDM fit gives $\Omega_\Lambda=0.6847\pm0.0073$, $0.1\sigma$ from~\eqref{eq:now}; the full-spectrum fit with
the derived baryon ratio (\S25.9) prefers $2.611\pm0.024$, $1.3\sigma$; DESI's $\Omega_m=0.2975\pm0.0086$ lies $2.2\sigma$ away,
as it does from Planck. The maximum-power rule (\S36.6) placed now within $3\%$ of the age; the resonance places it at the
measured value.
\sI\ Passing through resonance turns the phase of a driven mode by $\pi$: before now the horizon's mode runs ahead of the
rotation, after it runs behind. The present is that sign flip, the cosmic instance of the crossings at which a falling
rate meets a fixed one---the horizon of a black hole, where the time and radial directions exchange, and the conformal
reset, where one aeon's future is glued to the next one's past.
\sO\ The identification of the rotation's rate with $H_\infty$ was made with the measured $\Omega_\Lambda$ in view. It is recorded
as a hypothesis (H3), tested by the dark ratio as the CMB and BAO sharpen (register row 66); its grounds are set out in \S36.8,
and with the spin derived they reduce to the identification I8 (\S36.13), itself derived up to an integer that the data fix (\S36.14).
H3 is the opening principle's statement of the present, and with it the dark ratio needs no identification of its own (\S25.11).

\subsection*{36.8\quad Why the rotation turns at $H_\infty$}
\sD\ The constraint holds the phase at a constant rate (\S23), so the rate is a constant of the whole solution, and its value
can be read where the solution is simplest, in the final state. There nothing is left but the final horizon: matter has
thinned away, and the only scale is $H_\infty$, set by the boundary datum $N_\infty$ (\S44.6). The phase is an angle, so its
rate is a clock rate, and the final state offers one clock.

\sI\ That clock is the final horizon's own frequency, its surface gravity over $c$, $\kappa_\infty/c=H_\infty$. Two routes in the
framework give it. The horizon's temperature is $T_\infty=\hbar H_\infty/2\pi k_B$, and its thermal circle, the circle $S^1(2\pi)$ of
the electric coupling's calculation (\S43F), has the period $2\pi/H_\infty$: a phase in equilibrium with the horizon advances by
$2\pi$ per thermal period. And the final state's spatial spheres, at their narrowest of radius $c/H_\infty$, carry the conformal
scalar's lowest mode at frequency $c/R=H_\infty$, the mode that supplies the weight of the electric coupling's correction.

\sD\ With the rate fixed at $H_\infty$, the resonance of \S36.7 follows without further assumption: the present is the moment the
current horizon's $C_3$ cycle oscillates at the final horizon's own frequency, and $\Omega_\Lambda=27/4\pi^2$. \sO\ The equilibrium of
the phase with the final horizon is the one premise not yet derived from the action; the measured $\Omega_\Lambda$ was known when
it was formulated. The spinning-arc principle of \S36.9 gives the rate without it. Both routes above are read again in
\S36.10: the thermal circle is the turn of the final horizon's arcs, and its rate is fixed by the smoothness of the horizon; the
premise becomes I8, that the internal phase turns with that spin (\S36.13), which the quantum of the spin derives up to an integer
measured to be one (\S36.14).

\subsection*{36.9\quad The spinning-arc principle}
\sD\ (A hypothesis in release 1.11.0; derived for the final horizon in \S36.10--36.11.) The horizon is not a static sphere: it is swept out by C-arcs spinning about perpendicular axes, vertical and
horizontal, crossing one another (\S1). The spin is physical. Nothing acts on the arcs, so their rate is fixed, and on a
horizon, a surface receding at the speed of light, the arcs' rims move at $c$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\omega R=c .}
\label{eq:spin-arc}
\end{equation}
\sD\ \emph{The cosmic sphere.} The universe's horizon grows toward the radius at which the fixed spin's rims reach light
speed, and no further: $R_\infty=c/\omega$, so $H_\infty=\omega$ and
\begin{equation}
\Lambda=\frac{3\omega^2}{c^2} .
\end{equation}
The cosmological constant is the signature of the spin. The internal rotation is the spin itself (I8, \S36.13; derived in \S36.14),
so it turns at $H_\infty$, with no equilibrium premise, and the present is the moment the current horizon's $C_3$ cycle has slowed to the
spin, $\Omega_\Lambda=27/4\pi^2$ (\S36.7).

\sD\ \emph{The wobble meets the spin.} The threefold wobble of the arcs, which creates the three generator defects
(\S1; its count is the beat of \S1.11), is the horizon's $C_3$ mode: its frequency $(3\sqrt3/2\pi)\,c/R$ falls as the horizon grows, while the spin is fixed.
The present is the moment the wobble falls into step with the spin.

\emph{The spin is measured.} The birefringence of the CMB is the spin's phase accumulated since last scattering,
$\beta=\kappa\alpha\,\omega(t_0-t_{\rm rec})/2\pi$ (\S49.12), so the measured angle measures the spin: $0.277^\circ\pm0.057^\circ$
gives $\omega=(0.73\pm0.15)H_0$, against $H_\infty=0.827H_0$ from the expansion, $0.6\sigma$. Equivalently the twist of the oldest
light measures the cosmological constant, $\beta=(\kappa\alpha/2\pi)\sqrt{\Lambda c^2/3}\,(t_0-t_{\rm rec})$.

\sD\ \emph{The quantum sphere.} The same rule holds for a particle. A sphere swept at frequency $\omega$ has radius $c/\omega$;
with energy $\hbar\omega=mc^2$ that radius is $\hbar/mc$, the reduced Compton wavelength, and with the zero-point energy
$\hbar\omega/2$ it is half that. The internal clock de Broglie gave every particle, at $mc^2/\hbar$ \cite{deBroglie1925}, and the
light-speed trembling Schr\"odinger found in the electron's motion, at $2mc^2/\hbar$ and radius $\hbar/2mc$
\cite{Schrodinger1930,Hestenes1990}, are this rule at the smallest scale. From the electron to the edge of the universe
each closed sphere is swept by arcs whose rims move at light speed, and the Hubble quantum at the foot of the ladder,
$\hbar H_\infty$, is the cosmic sphere's quantum of spin (\S50.2).
\sP\ Register rows 67--71.

\subsection*{36.10\quad The spin derived: the thermal geometry of the final horizon}
\sS\ In the final state only the vacuum remains ($w=-1$, $V_0=0$, \S38), and the universe is de Sitter space with horizon
radius $R_\infty=c/H_\infty$. A static observer writes it, with $\varrho$ the distance from the observer and the horizon at
$\varrho=R_\infty$, as
\begin{equation}
ds^2=-\Big(1-\frac{\varrho^2}{R_\infty^2}\Big)c^2dT^2+\frac{d\varrho^2}{1-\varrho^2/R_\infty^2}+\varrho^2d\Omega^2 .
\label{eq:dS-static}
\end{equation}
Its thermal form, $T\to-i\tau$, is the four-sphere of radius $R_\infty$ \cite{GibbonsHawking1977}:
\begin{equation}
X_0+iX_4=\sqrt{R_\infty^2-\varrho^2}\;e^{iH_\infty\tau},\qquad \vec X=\varrho\,\hat n,\qquad X_0^2+X_4^2+|\vec X|^2=R_\infty^2 .
\label{eq:dS-sphere}
\end{equation}
Static time is an angle: advancing $\tau$ turns the four-sphere in the $(X_0,X_4)$ plane at the rate $H_\infty$, and the
horizon, $\varrho=R_\infty$, is the axis of the turn. \sD\ This turn is the spinning-arc picture, exactly.

\emph{The arcs.} Hold the angle and follow one line of sight, $\hat n$, through the observer to both sides: the points
$\vec X=s\,\hat n$, with $s$ running from $-R_\infty$ to $R_\infty$, form a semicircle of radius $R_\infty$ from a horizon point,
through the observer, to the opposite horizon point---a C-arc. The turn spins it about the axis through its two ends, and it
sweeps the two-sphere $X_0^2+X_4^2+s^2=R_\infty^2$. Lines of sight at right angles, vertical and horizontal, give arcs spinning
about perpendicular axes, and at each instant all the arcs cross at the observer. The two halves of each great circle, half a
turn apart, are a conjugate pair of C-arcs.

\emph{The rim moves at light speed.} A point of the arc at distance $\varrho$ from the observer runs on a circle of radius
$\sqrt{R_\infty^2-\varrho^2}$ at the rate $H_\infty$, so its speed is
\begin{equation}
v(\varrho)=c\sqrt{1-\varrho^2/R_\infty^2},
\label{eq:arc-speed}
\end{equation}
the rate at which a static clock at $\varrho$ runs: light speed at the observer, rest at the horizon, where time stands still.
The rim is the observer, and
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\omega=H_\infty,\qquad \omega R_\infty=c,\qquad \Lambda=\frac{3\omega^2}{c^2} .}
\label{eq:spin-derived}
\end{equation}
Every observer stands on the rim of the arcs of its own horizon.

\emph{The rate is fixed by the geometry.} At proper distance $\sigma$ from the horizon the plane of the turn is
$\sigma^2(H_\infty d\tau)^2+d\sigma^2$, a plane in polar coordinates with angle $H_\infty\tau$. It closes smoothly only if that angle
runs once around: the arcs meet the horizon without a defect only at the rate $H_\infty$, and a turn at any other rate $\omega'$
leaves at their ends a conical defect $2\pi(1-H_\infty/\omega')$. That defect would lie in the plane of the turn; the $C_3$ deficit
$\pi$ lies across it, on the horizon's sphere (\S7). The thermal period is one turn, $2\pi/\omega$, and the temperature of the final
horizon is the frequency of the turn over $2\pi$,
\begin{equation}
k_BT_\infty=\frac{\hbar\omega}{2\pi}.
\label{eq:spin-temperature}
\end{equation}

\emph{In real time.} The same Killing flow is a boost. The horizon's generators are light rays, and the flow advances their
rapidity at the rate $\kappa_\infty/c=H_\infty$, the surface gravity over $c$: in real time the rims of the arcs are the light rays
that make the horizon.

\emph{Why the spin is fixed.} \sS\ The surface gravity of a stationary horizon is the same over the whole horizon, the zeroth law
\cite{BCH1973}, so all the arcs turn together. And $\Lambda=3\omega^2/c^2$ cannot change while matter is separately conserved: the
contracted Bianchi identity, $\nabla^\mu G_{\mu\nu}=0$, with $\nabla^\mu T_{\mu\nu}=0$, forces $\partial_\nu\Lambda=0$. In the unimodular
form of gravity $\Lambda$ is a constant of integration, conjugate to the four-volume and conserved as energy is conserved by time
translation \cite{HenneauxTeitelboim1989}. \sD\ Nothing in the action exchanges energy with the vacuum: the scalar's vacuum constant
vanishes (\S38.6) and the rotation's constraint adds none (\S23.1). Nothing acts on the arcs, so their rate does not change. The
rate is the framework's boundary datum read as a frequency,
\begin{equation}
\omega\,t_{\rm P}=\frac{\lP}{R_\infty}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{N_\infty}}=9.66\times10^{-62},\qquad t_{\rm P}=\frac{\lP}{c},
\label{eq:spin-datum}
\end{equation}
so that ``reality is the spin'' and ``$N_\infty$ is the boundary datum'' (\S44.6) are one statement.

\emph{What is derived and what is not.} \sD\ For the final horizon the spinning-arc principle is a theorem of general relativity
read in the framework's geometry: the arcs, their perpendicular axes and their crossing at the observer, the fixed rate
$H_\infty$, the light-speed rim, $\Lambda=3\omega^2/c^2$, and the temperature as one turn. \sO\ The value of $\omega$ is not derived;
it is $N_\infty$ (\S44.6). The derivation holds only if the final state is de Sitter: evolving dark energy established at $5\sigma$
removes the final horizon and the fixed spin with it (register row~6). At every earlier epoch the current horizon has its own
rate $c/r=H(t)$, larger than the spin and falling toward it; on the current horizon the fixed spin's rim moves at
$\omega r=c\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda(t)}$, $0.82\,c$ today, and reaches $c$ only at $R_\infty$ (\S36.9).

\subsection*{36.11\quad The charge of the spin}
\sS\ The entropy of a Killing horizon is $2\pi$ times the Noether charge of its Killing field taken at unit surface gravity
\cite{Wald1993}. \sD\ For the final horizon that charge is the angular momentum of \S21, read at its radius:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{L=\frac{Ac^3}{8\pi G}=\MH\,c\,R=\frac{\hbar N}{4\pi},\qquad S=\frac{2\pi L}{\hbar},\qquad \EH=\omega L=k_BT\,S .}
\label{eq:spin-charge}
\end{equation}
\S21's $L_{\rm H}=\MH cr$, the horizon's mass carried around its radius at light speed, is the charge of the turn; the entropy is
the action of one turn in units of $\hbar$; and the exchange law of \S24, $\EH=k_BTS$, is the energy of a turn whose rim moves at
$c$, $E=\omega L$, the relation of light carried on a circle ($E=pc$, $L=pR$). The three were derived separately (\S20, \S21, \S24);
they are one statement about the spin. The same identities hold at every epoch for the current horizon with its own rate $c/r$.

\emph{The spin is not a rotation of space.} \sS\ A body with mechanical spin $s$ obeys a lower entropy bound,
$S\le2\pi\sqrt{(ER/c)^2-s^2}/\hbar$ \cite{Hod2000}: a rotation that carried the horizon's energy around at light speed, $s=ER/c$,
would leave it no entropy. \sD\ The horizon has the largest entropy its energy allows, $2\pi\EH r/\hbar c$ (\S34.5), and so no
mechanical spin: its spin is the turn of its time. This is the vorticity bound of Section~\ref{sec:rotation} seen from the other
side: the universe's rotation is internal because a rotation of space would cost the horizon its entropy. The Bekenstein bound
itself reads as a spin, $S\le2\pi L_{\max}/\hbar$, with $L_{\max}=ER/c$ the largest angular momentum an energy $E$ inside $R$ can carry,
reached only on a rim moving at light speed. With $S=2\pi L/\hbar$ and $E=\omega L$ the horizon saturates it exactly when $\omega R=c$:
the entropy alone fixes the rim.

\emph{The quantum of the spin.} \sD\ The turn is an angle of period $2\pi$, so the charge conjugate to it takes the values
$L=n\hbar$, and by~\eqref{eq:spin-charge}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{S=2\pi n,\qquad A=8\pi n\,\lP^2,\qquad N=4\pi n,\qquad \Delta E=\hbar\omega\ \text{per quantum}.}
\label{eq:area-quantum}
\end{equation}
Each quantum of spin adds $2\pi$ nats, $8\pi\lP^2$ of area and $4\pi\lP^2$ of effective area. \sI\ That is the surface of one Planck
sphere, the quantum sphere of the Planck mass, whose rim moves at light speed and which carries $\hbar$ (\S36.12): the horizon's spin
is the sum of the spins of the Planck spheres that tile its effective surface. \sD\ The identity $\hbar A_{\rm eff}/4\pi\lP^2=L$ holds
exactly when $A_{\rm eff}=2\pi r^2$; with the full area the sum would be twice the horizon's spin, so the additivity of spin is a
further route to the deficit $\pi$ (\S4.4, \S6, \S18.1). \sS\ The spacing $8\pi\lP^2$ is the one Bekenstein proposed for the horizon
area as an adiabatic invariant \cite{Bekenstein1974}; Maggiore obtained it from the damping of black-hole quasinormal modes
\cite{Maggiore2008}, Kothawala, Padmanabhan and Sarkar found the entropy step $2\pi$ in every Lanczos--Lovelock theory \cite{KPS2008},
and the periodicity of Euclidean time has been read as its origin \cite{Zeng2012}. Here it is the quantum of the spin. For the
cosmic horizon the quantum is $\hbar\omega=\hbar H_\infty=1.18\times10^{-33}$ eV, the Hubble quantum at the foot of the ladder (\S50.2),
and the final horizon holds $L_\infty/\hbar=N_\infty/4\pi=5.36\times10^{121}$ of them.

\subsection*{36.12\quad The quantum sphere and black holes}
\sD\ A rim moving at light speed carries $E=\omega L$, so a sphere whose energy turns at its own frequency carries $L=E/\omega$. For a
particle, $E=mc^2$. At de Broglie's clock, $\omega=mc^2/\hbar$, the rim is the Compton radius $\hbar/mc$ and $L=\hbar$. At the
zero-point energy, $mc^2=\tfrac12\hbar\omega$, the turn is Schr\"odinger's trembling at $2mc^2/\hbar$, the rim is $\hbar/2mc$, and
\begin{equation}
L=\frac{\hbar}{2}:
\label{eq:spin-half}
\end{equation}
the half of the zero-point energy is the half of the spin, the sign that needs $4\pi$ to close (\S1.8). \sS\ This is the
zitterbewegung reading of the electron's spin \cite{Hestenes1990}. For the Planck mass the rim is $\lP$ and $L=\hbar$: the Planck
sphere of \S36.11.

\sD\ A black hole is a closed sphere of the same kind. Its thermal form is a cigar whose turn has the rate $\omega=\kappa/c=c^3/4GM$
and whose widest circle, far from the hole, has radius $c/\omega=2r_s$, where the rim moves at light speed. Its entropy is again
$2\pi$ times the charge of the turn \cite{Wald1993}; a quantum $\hbar\omega=2\pi k_BT_{\rm BH}$ changes it by $2\pi$ through the first
law, so the area quantum is $8\pi\lP^2$ for every horizon, and a quantized hole absorbs and emits at $\omega_n=n\,\omega$, shifted by
$m\Omega_{\rm H}$ for a rotating hole \cite{CardosoFoitKleban2019,Agullo2021}. \sS\ The overtones of its ringing are damped in steps of
the same rate: the imaginary parts of the Schwarzschild quasinormal frequencies are spaced by $\kappa/c$ at high overtone
\cite{Nollert1993,Motl2003}. For a $60\,M_\odot$ remnant $\omega=846$ rad\,s$^{-1}$ ($135$ Hz), in the band where ringdowns are
measured; the LIGO--Virgo ringdowns give the area quantum $\alpha=15.6^{+20.5}_{-13.3}$ in units of $\lP^2$ \cite{Laghi2021}, against
$8\pi=25.1$, $+0.5\sigma$.

\subsection*{36.13\quad What the spin predicts}
\emph{The rotation turns with the spin.} \sI\ (I8) The internal phase of Section~\ref{sec:rotation} is the turn of \S36.10 read in real
time: its constraint~\eqref{eq:rot-constraint} holds $X_0=\Lambda/3$, the phase advances one radian per $e$-fold of the final
expansion, $\theta=\ln a$, and the rotation turns at $\omega=H_\infty$. The constraint carries no scale of its own (\S0.2), and of the
framework's lengths only $R_\infty$ gives a constant rate of the size the birefringence measures, so $X_0$ is a pure number $k$
times $\Lambda/3$, and the spin is $k=1$. \sM\ Two measurements bear on it. The birefringence gives $k=0.88\pm0.18$; with the present
at the resonance of \S36.7 (H3), Planck's $\Omega_\Lambda$ gives $k=0.9994\pm0.0053$. With I8 the equilibrium premise of \S36.8 is not
needed, and the resonance is the wobble meeting the spin. \sD\ The quantum of the spin fixes the rate up to an
integer, $\Omega_0=jH_\infty$, and the two measurements select $j=1$ (\S36.14): I8 is derived.

\emph{The phase turned since the beginning.} \sD\ With the rate $H_\infty$ and the present at the resonance, the phase turned since the
beginning is a pure number. In a flat universe of matter and vacuum $H_\infty t_0=\tfrac23\,\mathrm{artanh}\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda}$, so
\begin{equation}
\theta_0=\omega\,t_0=\tfrac23\,\mathrm{artanh}\,\frac{3\sqrt3}{2\pi}=0.7857\ \mathrm{rad},
\label{eq:theta0}
\end{equation}
and $0.78545$ with radiation, with no Hubble constant in it. \sO\ This lies $5\times10^{-5}$ rad from $\pi/4$, the half-apex angle of
the seed (\S3, \S6): the spin has turned an eighth of a turn since the beginning. The match was noticed after the computation;
for $\Omega_\Lambda$ anywhere in $[0.60,0.75]$ one this close to an angle $p\pi/q$ with $q\le12$ has a chance of $0.15\%$. No
derivation connects them, and were it exact it would place the present at $\Omega_\Lambda=0.68388$, $4\times10^{-5}$ from
$27/4\pi^2$, closer than any measurement can separate.

\emph{The birefringence in closed form.} \sP\ The CMB's polarization turns by the phase turned since last scattering, weighted by
the anomaly, $\beta=(\kappa\alpha/2\pi)\,\omega(t_0-t_{\rm rec})$ (\S49.12), so with $\kappa=6$
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\beta=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\,\mathrm{artanh}\,\frac{3\sqrt3}{2\pi}=0.3137^\circ}
\label{eq:beta-closed}
\end{equation}
($0.3136^\circ$ with radiation and the light's departure at recombination): the fine-structure constant, the anomaly of the cone
triples and the $C_3$ ratio, with nothing measured on the sky and no Hubble constant. The joint ACT and Planck angle,
$0.277^\circ\pm0.057^\circ$ \cite{Eskilt2026}, lies $0.6\sigma$ below. \S49.12 and \S49.13 give $0.314^\circ$ with the budget's expansion
history and the $C_3$ rate read at $H_0$; at the resonance budget both read $\Omega_\Lambda=27/4\pi^2$, and they agree within $0.003^\circ$.

\emph{The acceleration scale from the cosmological constant.} \sD\ At the present the $C_3$ rate is the spin, so the acceleration
scale of \S22 is set by $\Lambda$ alone:
\begin{equation}
a_0=\frac{\sqrt3}{\pi^2}\,cH_{\rm now}=\frac{2}{3\pi}\,c\,\omega=\frac{2}{3\pi}\,c^2\sqrt{\frac\Lambda3},
\label{eq:a0-Lambda}
\end{equation}
$1.149\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ with Planck's $\Lambda$. \sS\ Milgrom noted $a_0\approx c^2(\Lambda/3)^{1/2}$ up to a factor of
order one \cite{Milgrom1999}; the spin fixes the factor, $2/3\pi$. \sP\ It ties galaxies to the cosmological constant without a
Hubble constant (register row 71); it is not independent of rows 8 and 66.

\emph{Status.} Derived: the spin of the final horizon, its rate, its rim, its constancy, its charge and its quantum
(\S36.10--36.12), and the threefold count as a beat (\S1.11). Identified in release 1.13.0, derived in release 1.14.0 up to an integer
that the data fix (\S36.14): I8, the internal phase turning with the spin. Hypothesis: H3, the present as the resonance. Predictions:
register rows 68--73.

\subsection*{36.14\quad The quantum of the rotation is the quantum of the spin}
\sD\ The rate of the rotation is the one constant the action leaves free: the constraint~\eqref{eq:rot-constraint} holds
$\dot\theta=\Omega_0=c\sqrt{X_0}$ for any $X_0$, and I8 set $X_0=\Lambda/3$. The quantum of the spin fixes it up to an integer. On the
cosmological background the rotation sector vanishes on its solutions (\S23.1), so the energy of its dust in a comoving volume is its
Hamiltonian,
\begin{equation}
E=p_\theta\,\dot\theta=\Omega_0\,Q ,
\label{eq:rot-energy}
\end{equation}
with $Q=p_\theta$ the momentum conjugate to the phase, the charge of the conjugate pair. The phase is an angle, so $Q$ takes the values
$n\hbar$ and the dust comes in quanta of rest energy $\hbar\Omega_0$: the Josephson--Anderson relation of a condensate whose phase turns
at its chemical potential \cite{Anderson1966}. \sS\ A comoving quantum carries a Killing energy equal to its rest energy: in the flat
slicing of de~Sitter space the static observer's Killing field is $\xi=\partial_t-Hx^i\partial_i$, and $-u\cdot\xi=c^2$ per unit mass on
every comoving worldline. Every comoving quantum but the observer's own leaves the static patch, and the horizon takes up its energy as
heat: by the first law of the cosmological horizon \cite{GibbonsHawking1977}, energy $\delta E$ carried out across it raises its entropy
by $\delta S=\delta E/k_BT_\infty$. \sD\ The entropy of the final horizon changes only in steps of $2\pi$, one quantum of its spin
(\S36.11), so it takes up energy only in multiples of $2\pi k_BT_\infty=\hbar\omega$. Each quantum of the rotation therefore carries a
whole number of quanta of the spin:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\hbar\Omega_0=j\,\hbar\omega,\qquad \Omega_0=j\,H_\infty,\qquad X_0=j^2\,\frac{\Lambda}{3},\qquad j=1,2,3,\dots}
\label{eq:rot-quantum}
\end{equation}
($j=0$ is no rotation: the constraint's gradient would not be timelike.) The rate is a harmonic of the spin, fixed as Dirac's argument
fixes electric charge by the monopole \cite{Dirac1931}: a classical constant made consistent with a quantized boundary. \sS\ The same
structure holds a scalar condensate in equilibrium with a rotating black hole: its frequency is locked to the horizon's,
$\omega=m\Omega_{\rm H}$ with $m$ an integer \cite{HerdeiroRadu2014}. Here the integer counts the quanta of the spin carried by one quantum
of the rotation.

\sM\ The birefringence measures the integer. With the anomaly weight $\kappa=6$ of the cone fermions (\S49.12) it gives
$\Omega_0/H_\infty=0.88\pm0.18$: $j=1$ lies $0.7\sigma$ away, $j=2$ is excluded at $6.2\sigma$ and $j=0$ at $4.9\sigma$ (the angle alone
fixes $\kappa j=5.3\pm1.1$, and $\kappa=6$ is derived). With the present at the resonance (H3), Planck's $\Omega_\Lambda$ gives
$0.9994\pm0.0053$, independently of $\kappa$. Hence $j=1$: \emph{each quantum of the universe's rotation is one quantum of the final
horizon's spin}, and crossing the horizon it adds $2\pi$ to its entropy and $8\pi\lP^2$ to its area. I8 is derived up to an integer, and
the integer is measured, as the number of light neutrinos is (\S42.2). \sD\ The rotation's quanta sit at the floor of the final universe,
$\hbar\Omega_0/c^2=\hbar/cR_\infty$, the mass whose Compton length is the final horizon (\S48.1). The quantum is that of the charge: the
dust's density is the multiplier $\lambda$ of~\eqref{eq:rot-constraint}, which carries no gradient energy, so no de~Broglie length enters
its dynamics and its sound speed is zero on every scale (\S23.1), unlike a condensate of free particles of the same mass, whose Jeans
length would exceed the horizon. In the words of the opening principle: reality is the spin, the universe's rotation is made of its
quanta, and the present (H3) is the moment one quantum of the horizon's wobble equals one of them. \sP\ Register row~72.

\subsection*{36.15\quad The acceleration scale tracks the horizon, not the spin}
\sD\ The threshold of \S22.10 is one activation per period of the current horizon's fundamental $C_3$ mode,
$\tau_1=4\pi^2r/3\sqrt3\,c$, so the critical acceleration follows the horizon, $a_c\propto H(t)$, and at redshift $z$
\begin{equation}
a_0(z)=a_0\,E(z),\qquad E(z)=\frac{H(z)}{H_0} .
\label{eq:a0-z}
\end{equation}
The spin is fixed. The two meet only at the resonance, so the relations that equate them---$a_0=(2/3\pi)\,c^2\sqrt{\Lambda/3}$,
equation~\eqref{eq:a0-Lambda}, $\Omega_0=(3\pi/2)\,a_0/c$ (\S49.13), and with them $\beta=\tfrac92\alpha\,a_0t_0/c$---hold at the present
epoch, which is where they are tested. Were the spin to set the scale at every epoch, $a_0$ would not evolve. The baryon ratio of \S25.7
needs the same reading: the horizon's load $z_H=g_H/a_0$ is a pure number at every epoch only because $a_0$ tracks $H$.

\sM\ The data separate the two readings. A radial acceleration relation of $79$ star-forming galaxies at $0.33<z<1.44$ from the MUSE
Hubble Ultra Deep Field \cite{Ciocan2026} has a scale that rises with redshift: $1.99$, $2.25$, $2.48$ and $2.71\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$
in four bins near $z=0.47$, $0.70$, $0.95$ and $1.27$, and $2.38^{+0.12}_{-0.10}\times10^{-10}$ at $z\simeq1$; a constant scale is excluded.
With $a_0=1.148\times10^{-10}$ (the resonance budget, \S25.11), equation~\eqref{eq:a0-z} gives $1.49$, $1.72$, $2.00$ and $2.40$: the measured
values lie $33$, $31$, $24$ and $13$ per cent above, and within the survey the scale rises by a factor $1.36$ where $H(z)$ rises by $1.61$. The
survey's own linear fit extrapolates to $a_0(0)=1.00\pm0.04$, near the rotation-curve likelihood's $1.04$ (\S22.6), and its mass-to-light ratios
lie below the local calibration's: the offset and the slope fall on opposite sides of~\eqref{eq:a0-z}. Rotation curves at $z\simeq2$ all but
exclude a scale four times today's \cite{Milgrom2017}; \eqref{eq:a0-z} gives three. \sP\ A scale constant in time, or one departing from
$a_0E(z)$ at $5\sigma$ in a single method spanning $z=0$ to $1.5$, falsifies the horizon reading (register row~73).

\section{Dark Matter}\label{sec:DM}
% =====================================================================
\emph{Reading (Section~\ref{sec:rotation}).} In the rotation reading the condensate of this section is the
modulation of the universe's rotation (\S23.1), not a population of particles: the equations stand, and
their carrier is the rotation. The radial mode keeps its mass $m_\delta$ as an excitation of the field.


\subsection*{37.1\quad Residual modes}
The radial mode of the scalar invariant $\Xi=\phi_+\phi_-$ oscillates about the vacuum
$\Xi=2$.

\subsection*{37.2\quad Mass}
The potential $V=\tfrac12(\phi^2-2)^2+V_0$ has $V''(\sqrt2)=8$. \sD\ Canonically
normalized (\S41.4), the radial mode has $m_\delta^2c^2/\hbar^2=8\varepsilon_V/f_\delta^2$, so
it takes the form below exactly when the potential scale is the defect tension spread over
$L_*^2$, $\varepsilon_V=f_\delta^2/L_*^2$ \cite{TRSD2026}:
\begin{equation}
m_\delta=\frac{\sqrt8\,\hbar}{cL_*},\qquad L_*=\frac{8\pi GM_*}{c^2}=\frac{8\pi M_*c^2}{F},
\end{equation}
with $L_*$ the defect length of a reference mass $M_*$ (\S18.1). Hence the scale
duality
\begin{equation}
M_*\,m_\delta=\frac{\sqrt8}{8\pi}\,m_{\rm P}^2 .
\end{equation}
\calib $M_*=1.5\times10^{11}M_\odot$ gives $L_*=0.180$ pc and
$m_\delta c^2=1.003\times10^{-22}$ eV. The Lyman-$\alpha$ forest requires
$m_\delta c^2>2\times10^{-20}$ eV \cite{RogersPeiris2021}, which means
$M_*<7.5\times10^8M_\odot$: the reference mass is a dwarf-galaxy mass.
\sD\ The bound applies to this mode. It assumes \cite{RogersPeiris2021} a scalar that is all
the dark matter and evolves linearly as a free field of mass $m$. Here the condensate is the
whole of $\Omega_c$ (\S25); about the ring the potential is $4\eta^2+2\sqrt2\,\eta^3+\tfrac12\eta^4$
in field units, which shifts the oscillation frequency by $\Delta\omega/\omega=-\tfrac38\eta^2$,
below $4\times10^{-5}$ for the displacements $\eta\sim10^{-3}$--$10^{-2}$ of \S37.7 and falling
as $a^{-3}$, far from the large-misalignment regime that changes the transfer function
\cite{Leong2019}; the self-coupling is gravitational in strength (\S37.3); and a radial
oscillation at fixed phase does not populate the massless Goldstone mode. If the condensate
were only a fraction of the dark matter the bound would weaken \cite{Kobayashi2017,MixedFDM2026}.

\subsection*{37.3\quad Self-coupling}
\sD\ The coefficient $12$ of the quartic term is in field units. For the canonical field
$\chi=f_\delta\phi$ the quartic coupling is $\lambda=12\varepsilon_V/f_\delta^4$; with
$\varepsilon_V=f_\delta^2/L_*^2$ and, in natural units, $f_\delta^2=\delta M_{\rm P}^2/8\pi$,
\begin{equation}
\lambda=\frac{12\pi}{\delta}\,\frac{m_\delta^2}{M_{\rm P}^2},\qquad
g_\chi=\frac{\lambda\hbar^3}{8m_\delta^2c}=\frac{3\pi}{2\delta}\,\frac{\hbar^3}{m_{\rm P}^2c}
\ \stackrel{\delta=\pi}{=}\ \frac{3\hbar^3}{2m_{\rm P}^2c}=1.24\times10^{-95}\ \mathrm{J\,m^3},
\end{equation}
independent of $m_\delta$. (Versions up to 10 wrote $3\hbar^3/2m_\delta^2c$, larger by
$(m_{\rm P}/m_\delta)^2\sim10^{100}$.) In a halo of density $10^{-21}\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ the
self-interaction energy is $2\times10^{-9}$ of the gradient energy at 1 kpc: the condensate is
effectively free.

\subsection*{37.4\quad Gross--Pitaevskii}
\begin{equation}
i\hbar\,\partial_t\psi=-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m_\delta}\nabla^2\psi+m_\delta\Phi\psi+g_\chi|\psi|^2\psi .
\end{equation}

\subsection*{37.5\quad Quantum pressure}
\begin{equation}
Q=-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m_\delta^2}\,\frac{\nabla^2\sqrt{\rho_\chi}}{\sqrt{\rho_\chi}} .
\end{equation}

\subsection*{37.6\quad Solitonic cores}
$\lambda_{\rm dB}=2\pi\hbar/m_\delta v=0.60$ kpc for $10^{-22}$ eV at
$200\ \mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, scaling as $1/m_\delta$; at the Lyman-$\alpha$ bound,
$2\times10^{-20}$ eV, it is $3.0$ pc, so the predicted cores are parsec-scale.

\subsection*{37.7\quad The initial displacement}
\sS\ The relic density of the condensate is fixed by misalignment \cite{HOTW2017}: a field of mass $m$
displaced by $\phi_i$ starts oscillating at $3H=m$ and dilutes as $a^{-3}$ afterwards,
giving, in the sudden-onset estimate,
\begin{equation}
\Omega_ch^2\simeq0.12\left(\frac{\phi_i}{8.5\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{GeV}}\right)^{2}
\left(\frac{m}{10^{-22}\ \mathrm{eV}}\right)^{1/2}.
\label{eq:misalign}
\end{equation}
Requiring the observed $\Omega_ch^2=0.120$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
$m$ & $\phi_i$ required & in units of $f_\delta=M_{\rm P}/\sqrt8=4.32\times10^{18}$ GeV \\
\midrule
$10^{-22}$ eV & $8.5\times10^{16}$ GeV & $2.0\times10^{-2}$ \\
$2\times10^{-20}$ eV (Lyman-$\alpha$) & $2.3\times10^{16}$ GeV & $5.3\times10^{-3}$ \\
$10^{-18}$ eV & $8.5\times10^{15}$ GeV & $2.0\times10^{-3}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The exact radiation-era solution, $\phi=\phi_i\Gamma(\tfrac54)(mt/2)^{-1/4}J_{1/4}(mt)$,
leaves $0.403$ times the sudden-onset relic density, so each $\phi_i$ above rises by a
factor $1.576$ ($1.35\times10^{17}$ GeV at $10^{-22}$ eV); the conclusion is unchanged.

Conversely, a geometric displacement $\phi_i=\sqrt2f_\delta$ would require
$m=3.8\times10^{-30}$ eV, ten orders of magnitude below the Lyman-$\alpha$ bound and in
the range where the field acts as dark energy rather than dark matter.

\emph{Constraint.} The condensate can be the dark matter only if its initial
displacement is $10^{-3}$ to $10^{-2}$ of the defect scale. The geometry supplies no
such suppression, so this is a second calibration in the dark sector alongside $L_*$,
and a target for the same reset calculation that would give the baryon ratio (\S25.7).

% =====================================================================
\subsection*{37.8\quad The dual of the horizon mass}
\sD\ Evaluated at the horizon mass $M_{\rm H}=c^3/2GH_0$, the scale duality gives
\begin{equation}
m_Uc^2=\frac{\sqrt8}{8\pi}\,\frac{m_{\rm P}^2c^2}{M_{\rm H}}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2\pi}\,\hbar H_0
=0.2251\,\hbar H_0 ,
\end{equation}
$3.2\times10^{-34}$ eV at $H_0=67.35$: the dual of the whole horizon has the Hubble mass scale.

\section{Dark Energy}\label{sec:DE}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{38.1\quad Vacuum defect}
The horizon defect spread through the three dimensions of space (\S26.4).

\subsection*{38.2\quad Equation of state}
$w=-n/3$ with $n=3$, so $w=-1$.

\subsection*{38.3\quad Density}
\begin{equation}
\rho_\Lambda=\Omega_\Lambda\,\rho_c=\Omega_\Lambda\,\frac{3H_0^2}{8\pi G}
=\Omega_\Lambda\,\frac{3F}{8\pi c^2r^2},\qquad
\rho_\Lambda c^2=5.24\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}\ \text{(Planck)}.
\end{equation}

\subsection*{38.4\quad Relation to $a_0$}
Eliminating $H_0$ with $a_0=(\sqrt3/\pi^2)cH_0$:
\begin{equation}
\rho_\Lambda c^2=\Omega_\Lambda\,\frac{\pi^3}{8}\,\frac{a_0^2}{G},
\end{equation}
an energy density: $a_0^2/G$ has units of $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$.

\subsection*{38.5\quad Ratio}
$\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_c=5/2$ (\S25).

\predict $w=-1$ exactly. The DESI DR2 BAO analysis preferred evolving dark energy at
$2.8$--$4.2\sigma$ \cite{DESI2025}; adding the Lyman-$\alpha$ full shape, the preference is
$2.7\sigma$ with the CMB and $3.2\sigma$ with supernovae \cite{DESI2026lya}. The next releases
test this directly.

\subsection*{38.6\quad The vacuum constant vanishes}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:V0}
\sD\ With $\varepsilon_V=f_\delta^2/L_*^2$ (\S37.2), a vacuum constant $V_0$ of order one
exceeds the observed vacuum energy by more than $10^{21}$; the vacuum energy is carried by
the space-filling defect of \S38.1, not by $V_0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At $\delta=\pi$, $f_\delta^2=F/8=1.51\times10^{43}$ N. For $L_*=0.180$ pc,
$\varepsilon_V=4.9\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$, so $V_0=2$ gives
$9.8\times10^{11}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$ against $\rho_\Lambda c^2=5.2\times10^{-10}\
\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$, a factor $1.9\times10^{21}$. The Lyman-$\alpha$ bound shortens $L_*$ below
$9.0\times10^{-4}$ pc and raises $\varepsilon_V$ above $1.9\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$,
so $V_0<3\times10^{-26}$.
\end{proof}
GRUT therefore takes $V_0=0$, the choice already made at the reset.

\section{The CMB}\label{sec:CMB}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{39.1\quad Acoustic peaks from the sound cone}
The defect cone is the sound cone (\S4.4). Before recombination the photon--baryon
fluid carries sound at $c_s=c/\sqrt{3(1+R)}$, with $R$ the baryon loading; at $R=0$
this is the $h=\sqrt3\,r$ cone. The comoving sound horizon $r_s=\int c_s\,dt/a$ at
recombination subtends $\theta_*=r_s/D_M=0.0104109$ \cite{Planck2018}, which sets the
acoustic scale $\ell_A=\pi/\theta_*\approx302$ and places the first peak at
$\ell\approx220$.

\subsection*{39.2\quad Selection rule}
Three defects at $120^\circ$ on the equator of the horizon sum as
$\sum_je^{-im\phi_j}=3$ when $3\mid m$ and $0$ otherwise, and their reflection symmetry
contributes $(-1)^{\ell+m}$:
\begin{equation}
b_{\ell m}=0\quad\text{unless}\quad m\equiv0\pmod3\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ell+m\ \text{even}.
\end{equation}

\subsection*{39.3\quad The full spectrum and $C_3/C_2$}
By the addition theorem
$\sum_mY_{\ell m}(\hat n_j)Y^*_{\ell m}(\hat n_k)=\tfrac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}P_\ell(\hat n_j\cdot\hat n_k)$,
the three defects give
\begin{equation}
C_\ell=\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\sum_m|b_{\ell m}|^2
=\frac{3A^2\delta_j^2}{4\pi}\Big[1+2P_\ell\big(-\tfrac12\big)\Big],
\label{eq:Cl}
\end{equation}
since $\hat n_j\cdot\hat n_k=1$ three times and $-\tfrac12$ six times. With
$P_2(-\tfrac12)=-\tfrac18$ and $P_3(-\tfrac12)=\tfrac7{16}$,
\begin{equation}
\frac{C_3}{C_2}=\frac{1+\tfrac78}{1-\tfrac14}=\frac52 ,
\end{equation}
and the dipole vanishes, $C_1=0$. The formula gives every multipole of the defect
pattern:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccccccc@{}}
\toprule
$\ell$ & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 & 9 & 10 \\
\midrule
$C_\ell/C_2$, defect pattern & 1 & 2.50 & 0.563 & 1.09 & 2.20 & 0.738 & 1.14 & 2.05 & 0.831 \\
$C_\ell/C_2$, primordial plateau & 1 & 0.50 & 0.300 & 0.200 & 0.143 & 0.107 & 0.083 & 0.067 & 0.055 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The defect pattern adds to the primordial plateau ($C_\ell\propto1/\ell(\ell+1)$) with
an amplitude $\varepsilon$ to be fitted, and the observed map carries it smoothed by the
transfer function. The mod-3 / parity search at $\ell=4$--$10$ on the Planck Commander
map found no excess over the isotropic expectation, which bounds $\varepsilon$ for the
point-source pattern. Under isotropy the share of power in the admissible modes
($3\mid m$, $\ell+m$ even, about one axis) has mean
$f_\ell=|\mathcal M_\ell|/(2\ell+1)=0.111,\,0.182,\,0.231,\,0.133,\,0.176,\,0.211,\,0.143$
for $\ell=4,\dots,10$; a defect component of fraction $\varepsilon$ raises it to
$\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)f_\ell$, so the null result bounds $\varepsilon$ at those
multipoles. The smoothed pattern is the next target (Appendix~\ref{sec:next}).

\subsection*{39.4\quad Octupole handedness}
The two-C/S structure carries a handedness. For a real sky
$a_{\ell,-m}=(-1)^ma^*_{\ell m}$, so the moduli $|a_{3,\pm3}|$ are equal and the
handedness lives in the phase: the orientation of the triangular octupole, and its
parity-odd counterpart in polarization, the $EB$ correlation. Isotropic cosmic
birefringence is measured at $\beta=0.34^\circ\pm0.09^\circ$ \cite{EskiltKomatsu2022};
deriving the sign and size of $\beta$ from the two-C/S rotation is the test (Appendix~\ref{sec:next}).

\subsection*{39.5\quad Quadrupole--octupole alignment}
The defects fix one axis: about it the quadrupole is $m=0$ and the octupole
$m=\pm3$. Because the usual alignment statistic (the axis maximizing
$\sum_m m^2|a_{\ell m}|^2$) points $90^\circ$ away from the axis of a pure $m=0$
quadrupole, the defect axis is best compared with the octupole axis directly.

% =====================================================================
\subsection*{39.6\quad The projected defect pattern}
\sD\ Place the three defects at distance $D$ and project their potential onto the
last-scattering sphere of radius $r_{\rm LS}$. For $r_{\rm LS}<D$,
\begin{equation*}
\frac{1}{|r_{\rm LS}\hat n-D\hat n_j|}=\sum_\ell\frac{r_{\rm LS}^\ell}{D^{\ell+1}}\,\frac{4\pi}{2\ell+1}
\sum_mY_{\ell m}(\hat n)Y^*_{\ell m}(\hat n_j),
\end{equation*}
so
\begin{equation}
C_\ell^{\rm proj}\propto\frac{(r_{\rm LS}/D)^{2\ell}}{(2\ell+1)^2}\Big[1+2P_\ell\big(-\tfrac12\big)\Big],
\end{equation}
with $D/r_{\rm LS}$ in place of $r_{\rm LS}/D$ when the defects lie inside the sphere. The
selection rule is untouched---projection acts on $\ell$, not on $m$---and the ratios fall:
$C_3/C_2=\tfrac52(\tfrac57)^2(r_{\rm LS}/D)^2=1.276\,(r_{\rm LS}/D)^2$.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
\toprule
$D/r_{\rm LS}$ & $C_3/C_2$ & $C_4/C_2$ & $C_5/C_2$ & $C_6/C_2$ & $C_7/C_2$ \\
\midrule
$1$ & $1.276$ & $0.174$ & $0.226$ & $0.325$ & $0.082$ \\
$1.25$ & $0.816$ & $0.071$ & $0.059$ & $0.054$ & $0.009$ \\
$1.5$ & $0.567$ & $0.034$ & $0.020$ & $0.013$ & $0.001$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The admissible-power test at $\ell=4$--$10$ on the Commander maps tests $m$ only and is
unchanged; the projection adds a prediction for how the defect component falls with $\ell$. A
physical defect profile and the integrated Sachs--Wolfe effect of a decaying potential remain.

\section{Black Holes and Information}\label{sec:BH}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{40.1\quad Extreme cones}
\sS\ For radial light in Schwarzschild spacetime, $dr/dt=\pm c(1-r_s/r)$, which vanishes at
$r=r_s$. The cones close up at the horizon, and inside it every future cone points to
$r=0$.

\subsection*{40.2\quad Horizon}
$r_s=2GM/c^2=2c^2M/F$; for the cosmic horizon, $r_s(\MH)=r$.

\subsection*{40.3\quad Entropy}
$S=A/4\lP^2$ with $A=4\pi r_s^2$; for the cosmic horizon, $S=N/2$.

\subsection*{40.4--40.5\quad Information and the Page curve}
The cell count is preserved by the global structure. The semiclassical ``island''
calculations reproduce the unitary Page curve of an evaporating black hole
\cite{Penington2020,AEMM2019,AMMZ2020}, which is the result GRUT's information
conservation requires.

% =====================================================================
\section{Gravity and the Scalar Action}\label{sec:action}
% =====================================================================

\subsection*{41.1--41.2\quad The cone at every scale}
One cone governs every scale. Its opening is the light cone (\S1.10, \S28), its
deficit is curvature (\S1.7, \S30), its modes are quantum amplitudes (\S32), and its
horizon cells are information (\S20).

\subsection*{41.3\quad Graviton}
A massless spin-2 field coupled to energy--momentum must couple universally (Weinberg
\cite{Weinberg1964}), and with consistent self-coupling it reproduces general
relativity (Deser \cite{Deser1970}).

\subsection*{41.4\quad The gravitational--scalar action}
\begin{gather}
S=\int d^4x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{c^4R}{16\pi G}
-\frac{\delta c^4}{16\pi G}\,g^{\mu\nu}\partial_\mu\phi_+\partial_\nu\phi_-
-\varepsilon_V\,V(\Xi)\right],\\
\Xi=\phi_+\phi_-,\qquad V(\Xi)=\tfrac12(\Xi-2)^2+V_0 .
\end{gather}
With the axiom, $c^4/16\pi G=F/16\pi$, so the gravitational and kinetic coefficients
are $F/16\pi$ and $\delta F/16\pi$. The potential enters with a minus sign
($\mathcal L=T-V$) and carries the energy-density scale $\varepsilon_V$. Canonically
normalizing the radial mode ties that scale to the dark-matter mass:
$m_\delta^2c^2/\hbar^2=4\varepsilon_V/K$, with $K=\delta F/16\pi=f_\delta^2/2$; with
$m_\delta=\sqrt8\hbar/cL_*$ this is $\varepsilon_V=f_\delta^2/L_*^2$ (\S37.2). The vacuum
energy $\varepsilon_VV_0$ vanishes (Theorem~\ref{thm:V0}). The action covers gravity and the
defect scalar; electromagnetic, fermionic and quantum structure enter as standard
physics (\S0.8), and \S54.5 writes them with it as one action.

\subsection*{41.6\quad Two readings of the pair}
Two structural readings of $\phi_\pm$ are worth recording, both consistent with \S1.8.

\emph{Spinor.} The conjugate pair are the two chiralities: the exchange
$\phi_+\leftrightarrow\phi_-$ is $\sigma_1$, a $2\pi$ rotation exchanges the lobes and
$4\pi$ restores them, which is the double cover $SU(2)\to SO(3)$ of \S1.8 and the
signature of spin-$\tfrac12$. Then $\Xi=\phi_+\phi_-$ is the Dirac mass bilinear, and
$V(\Xi)$ is a potential for it, of Nambu--Jona-Lasinio type: the mass is a condensate and
$V''=8$ is the gap. Making this a derivation requires first-order (Dirac) equations with
a tetrad; the cone supplies two null directions, which is half of one.

\emph{String.} The kinetic normalization $f_\delta^2=\delta c^4/8\pi G$ has the units of a
tension, $\mathrm{J\,m^{-1}}$, and is the only tension in the framework. Read as a
worldsheet tension $T=f_\delta^2$ it gives the string length
$\ell_s^2=\hbar c/2\pi T=4\hbar G/\pi c^3=(4/\pi)\,\lP^2$ at $\delta=\pi$, of
order the Planck area, with the pair as the left- and right-moving modes. This is a
dimensional identification, not a derivation of a string theory.

\subsection*{41.5\quad The two equations}
Geometry: $h=r$. Closure: $V_s=4V_c$ (\S3.12) and $4\dtot=4\pi$ (\S4.4).


% =====================================================================
\subsection*{41.7\quad The fermion and gauge sectors}
\sD\ Spinors exist on the geometry: $S^3\cong SU(2)$ double-covers $SO(3)$ (\S1.8--1.9), so
the fermion sector is
\begin{equation}
S_\psi=\int d^4x\,e\,\Big[\bar\psi_k\,i\gamma^ae_a^{\ \mu}\Big(\partial_\mu+\tfrac14\omega_\mu^{\ bc}\gamma_{bc}\Big)\psi_k-\big(\bar\psi_{Lj}\,M_{jk}\,\psi_{Rk}+{\rm h.c.}\big)\Big],
\end{equation}
with tetrad $e_a^{\ \mu}$ and spin connection $\omega_\mu^{\ bc}$. The $C_3$ symmetry of generation
space makes the square-root mass matrix circulant, $\sqrt M=a\,\mathbb 1+b\,P+b^*P^2$ with $P$ the
cyclic shift and $b=|b|e^{i\delta}$; its eigenvalues are $a+2|b|\cos(\delta+2\pi k/3)$, with sum
$3a$ and sum of squares $3a^2+6|b|^2$. The seed cone $h=r$ is $|b|=a/\sqrt2$, and then
\begin{equation}
Q=\frac{\sum_km_k}{\big(\sum_k\sqrt{m_k}\big)^2}=\frac{3a^2+6|b|^2}{9a^2}=\frac23
\end{equation}
identically in $\delta$, for signed roots: Koide's relation is derived from the $C_3$ symmetry and
the seed cone, and a negative root is the conjugate branch of the neutrinos (I7).

\sD\ The defect $U(1)$ is not electromagnetism. Were the condensate electrically charged, the
Higgs mechanism would give the photon a mass $m_\gamma=\sqrt2\,e\,f_\delta\phi_0\sim10^{18}$ GeV,
against the bound $m_\gamma<10^{-18}$ eV \cite{PDG2024}. Electromagnetism therefore enters as an
independent $U(1)$, $S_A=-\tfrac14\int d^4x\,e\,F_{ab}F^{ab}$ on the same tetrad, with the
$\psi_k$ charged under it; its charges are derived in \S31.4 and the value of its coupling is \S43F.

\section{Three Generations on the Seed Cone}\label{sec:generations}
% =====================================================================
The same two primitives that build the cosmology---the seed cone $h=r$ and the three
$C_3$ generators---have a second home in particle physics. The Standard Model has
exactly three generations of fermions. Placing them on the seed cone yields three
predictions for the lepton sector, each testable by experiments running now.

\subsection*{42.1\quad Generation space and the seed cone}
Represent a fermion family by the vector of square-root masses in generation space,
\begin{equation}
\mathbf v=(\sqrt{m_1},\sqrt{m_2},\sqrt{m_3}).
\end{equation}
The $C_3$ that cycles the three generators cycles the three axes, and its fixed
direction is the axis $\hat u=(1,1,1)/\sqrt3$. The seed cone about $\hat u$ is the set
of vectors at $45^\circ$ to it: the cone $h=r$, with axial and radial components
equal. The angle $\theta$ between $\mathbf v$ and $\hat u$ obeys
\begin{equation}
\cos^2\theta=\frac{(\mathbf v\cdot\hat u)^2}{|\mathbf v|^2}
=\frac{\big(\sum_k\sqrt{m_k}\big)^2}{3\sum_km_k}=\frac{1}{3Q},\qquad
Q\equiv\frac{\sum_km_k}{\big(\sum_k\sqrt{m_k}\big)^2}.
\label{eq:koideangle}
\end{equation}
So the family lies on the seed cone, $\theta=45^\circ$, exactly when
\begin{equation}
Q=\frac23 .
\label{eq:koide}
\end{equation}
\sI\ (I6) This is Koide's relation \cite{Koide1983}. Foot observed that it states a $45^\circ$
angle to the axis $(1,1,1)$ \cite{Foot1994}; GRUT obtains it as the seed condition in
generation space: the $45^\circ$ cone is the light cone $h=r$ of \S1.10. \sD\ The cycle supplies the form in which
the cone is a light cone. With $L$ the Laplacian of the $C_3$ cycle (\S9), whose doublet eigenvalue is $3/\ell^2$,
\begin{equation}
Q(\mathbf x)=\Big(\sum_nx_n\Big)^2-\ell^2\,\mathbf x^{\mathsf T}L\,\mathbf x=3\big(|\mathbf x_s|^2-|\mathbf x_d|^2\big)
\end{equation}
has signature $(1,2)$, singlet against doublet, and Koide's relation is $Q(\sqrt{\mathbf m})=0$: the seed condition
in the cycle's own form. Equivalently, $\sum_{\rm edges}(\sqrt{m_n}-\sqrt{m_{n+1}})^2=(\sum_n\sqrt{m_n})^2$, which the
charged leptons satisfy as $2824.640$ against $2824.658$ MeV, a difference of $6.6\times10^{-6}$ within the error of
$m_\tau$. \sI\ What remains identified is that the $\sqrt{m}$ vector obeys the seed condition. (The seed condition follows
from the vacuum of the axiom's potential, given each generation's amplitude as its generator's invariant raised by the pair, \S42.8.) The three
generators at $120^\circ$ parametrize the cone:
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{m_k}=\mu\Big[1+\sqrt2\cos\Big(\delta+\frac{2\pi k}{3}\Big)\Big],\qquad k=0,1,2 .
\label{eq:koidepar}
\end{equation}
The axial component of $\mathbf v$ is $\mu\sqrt3$, and the radial component is
$\mu\sqrt2\cdot\sqrt{3/2}=\mu\sqrt3$. They are equal, which is $h=r$. The scale is
$\mu$, and $\delta$ is the orientation of the generator triangle on the cone.

For the charged leptons (PDG masses \cite{PDG2024}),
\begin{equation}
Q_\ell=0.6666645\pm0.0000051,\qquad \theta_\ell=44.99991^\circ,
\end{equation}
with $\mu_\ell^2=313.851$ MeV and $\delta_\ell=0.22224$ (the $\tau$ at $k=0$), within
$2\times10^{-5}$ of $\tfrac29$ \cite{Brannen2006}. The
charged-lepton family lies on the seed cone to one part in $10^5$.

\subsection*{42.2\quad The group structure}
The primitives of \S1 map onto the structure of the Standard Model:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
GRUT primitive & Group & Standard Model role \\
\midrule
conjugate pair, glued (\S1.8) & $S^3\cong SU(2)$ & weak isospin doublets, e.g.\ $(\nu,e)$ \\
three generators (\S4.1) & $\mathbb Z_3$ & three generations; centre of colour $SU(3)$ \\
continuous phase (\S31) & $U(1)$ & hypercharge, electromagnetism \\
tetrahedral closure (\S4.4) & $A_4$ & the flavour group of tri-maximal mixing \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Colour-singlet families carry no $\mathbb Z_3$ triality and lie on the seed cone.
Quark families carry triality and lie off it: with PDG running masses the up-type
family sits at $51.2^\circ$ ($Q=0.849$) and the down-type family at $47.5^\circ$
($Q=0.731$). Exactly three generators means exactly three light neutrinos, in
agreement with the $Z$ width, $N_\nu=2.996\pm0.007$ \cite{JanotJadach2020}.

\subsection*{42.3\quad Prediction 1: the tau mass}
Given $m_e$ and $m_\mu$, the seed cone~\eqref{eq:koide} fixes the third charged
lepton:
\begin{equation}
m_\tau=1776.969\ \mathrm{MeV}.
\end{equation}
The measured value is $1776.93\pm0.09$ MeV \cite{PDG2024}, $0.4\sigma$ away. The
uncertainty of the prediction from $m_e$ and $m_\mu$ is below $10^{-3}$ MeV, so the
test is limited only by the tau-mass measurement.

\emph{Test.} BESIII, Belle II, and a future super tau--charm factory aiming at
$\pm0.01$--$0.05$ MeV. A converged value more than $3\sigma$ from $1776.969$ MeV
falsifies the seed cone in generation space. The relation holds for pole masses;
explaining why the pole mass is the right one is part of the open work.

\subsection*{42.4\quad Prediction 2: the neutrino masses}
The lepton doublet $(\nu,e)$ is the conjugate pair, so its two members share the seed
cone: $Q_\nu=\tfrac23$. With the measured splittings,
$\Delta m^2_{21}=(7.53\pm0.10)\times10^{-5}$ eV$^2$ and
$\Delta m^2_{31}=(2.529\pm0.021)\times10^{-3}$ eV$^2$ (NuFIT~6.1, including JUNO
\cite{NuFIT61,JUNO2026}), the cone
fixes the absolute masses. With all components positive, $Q_\nu$ cannot reach
$\tfrac23$: its maximum is $0.585$ in normal and $0.500$ in inverted ordering. The
solution puts the lightest state on the conjugate branch, $\sqrt{m_k}<0$ in
equation~\eqref{eq:koidepar}. That is allowed, since the physical mass is the square,
and it is the double cone of the conjugate pair. Brannen first applied Koide's
relation to neutrinos this way \cite{Brannen2006}. Each ordering gives one discrete
spectrum:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
\toprule
Branch & $m_1$ & $m_2$ & $m_3$ & $\sum m_\nu$ & $m_\beta$ & $m_{\beta\beta}$ \\
\midrule
normal & $0.365$ & $8.685$ & $50.29$ & $59.34$ & $8.97$ & $1.33$--$4.07$ \\
inverted & $49.165$ & $49.925$ & $2.917$ & $102.01$ & $48.85$ & $17.2$--$48.4$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

{\footnotesize All masses in meV. The $m_{\beta\beta}$ range spans the unknown
Majorana phases, using the mixing of \S42.5.}
\end{center}
The $1\sigma$ uncertainty of the splittings moves $\sum m_\nu$ by about $\pm0.2$ meV.

\emph{Tests.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Ordering.} NuFIT~6.1 prefers the normal ordering at $\Delta\chi^2=9.4$
\cite{NuFIT61}; JUNO resolves it within a few years.
\item \emph{Cosmology.} DESI DR2 with the CMB bounds $\sum m_\nu<64$ meV at $95\%$ in
$\Lambda$CDM \cite{DESI2025}, which favours the normal branch. Coming surveys with
$\sigma(\sum m_\nu)\approx15$--$20$ meV separate $59.3$ from $102.0$.
