speed of the field around a system, $\propto a^{-1}$ for a relativistic-born component and
$\sigma_q\propto a^{-3/4}$ for the cold condensate; $|\Phi_{\rm bar}|$, the depth of the baryonic
well; and $g_{\rm top}$. The formulation separates young from mature systems: where
$v(a)^2\gg2|\Phi_{\rm bar}|$ the capture fraction collapses and the dynamics are baryonic, and where
$v(a)^2\ll2|\Phi_{\rm bar}|$ it converges to one and the full boost switches on.

\subsection*{47.5\quad Galaxy clusters}
\sM\ Clusters test where the bound condensate sits. For a representative rich cluster---a
$\beta$-model atmosphere with $\beta=\tfrac23$, core radius $200$ kpc, central density
$4.5\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and temperature $6$ keV, gas fraction $0.116$ at $1.2$ Mpc,
stars adding $12\%$ of the gas---the complete equation with full capture gives, against the
hydrostatic mass,
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
\toprule
$r$ [kpc] & $250$ & $500$ & $1000$ & $1500$ \\
\midrule
$g_{\rm bar}/a_0$ & $0.101$ & $0.094$ & $0.065$ & $0.048$ \\
$M_{\rm GRUT}/M_{\rm HSE}$ & $0.28$ & $0.38$ & $0.55$ & $0.68$ \\
with member halos & $0.32$--$0.36$ & $0.42$--$0.48$ & $0.61$--$0.68$ & $0.75$--$0.83$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\sD\ Two things differ from MOND. Member galaxies keep the halos they captured before infall,
because the bound condensate obeys the equivalence principle and there is no external-field effect
(\S46.5); with halos of $4.5$--$10$ times the stellar mass within $50$ kpc they add $4$--$15\%$ of
the hydrostatic mass. And the condensate is not scarce: on cluster scales it is pressureless
(\S47.8), so a cluster collapses with its cosmic share, $\Omega_c/\Omega_b=5.35$ times its baryons,
close to what the hydrostatic masses require ($7.7$ times the baryons at $1.2$ Mpc in this model,
against $6.35$ for the cosmic share). The bound-condensate law places that share too far out: the
polarization profile $\rho\propto r^{-2}$ gives $M(<r)\propto r$, and the reservoir radius of
equation~\eqref{eq:reservoir} lies near $1.9$ Mpc. \sD\ The cluster problem is one of arrangement (resolved by the relaxation state of \S47.6--47.7: a cluster assembled at $z_f=0.5$ carries $0.74$--$0.80$ of its hydrostatic mass),
not of amount: inside $1$ Mpc the bound law falls short by a factor of $1.5$ (with member halos, at
$1$ Mpc) to $3.6$ (at $250$ kpc). It closes if infalling condensate reaches the polarization
arrangement more slowly than a cluster assembles, so that clusters, the youngest bound systems,
still carry a collapsed and centrally concentrated halo. The relaxation time from infall to the
polarization equilibrium is the calculation that decides it; \S47.6 formulates it as the
relaxation state.

\subsection*{47.6\quad The relaxation state: support, resistance and the cluster--galaxy difference}
The bound-condensate law (Theorem~\ref{thm:boundlaw}) describes the end state of a system's
condensate, not every state it passes through. A system's condensate occupies one of three
configurations, and its position among them is its relaxation state.

\begin{definition}[Support, infall and resistance]\label{def:levels}
\sD\ (i) The \emph{support level} is the polarization equilibrium of Theorem~\ref{thm:boundlaw}: the
bound condensate is distributed as the phantom density, the halo is isothermal, $\rho\propto r^{-2}$,
and the rotation curve is flat. (ii) The \emph{infall configuration} is the condensate as it arrives:
on scales above $\lambda_J$ it is pressureless (\S47.8), so a collapsing system gathers its cosmic
share, $\Omega_c/\Omega_b$ times its baryons, into a collapsed, centrally concentrated halo. (iii) The
\emph{resistance level} is the absence of condensate: $g=g_{\rm bar}$ and Keplerian curves, reached
only where none is bound---below $\lambda_J$ (\S46.3) or where the capture fraction vanishes
(\S47.2), as in the Solar System.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Relaxation state]\label{def:relax}
The relaxation state of a bound system is the polarized fraction $f\in[0,1]$ of its condensate,
\begin{equation}
M_\chi(<r)=f\,M_{\rm pol}(<r)+(1-f)\,M_{\rm inf}(<r).
\label{eq:relax-mix}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\sD\ The resistance level is not the unrelaxed state of a bound system: a system that has captured
its condensate cannot return to Newton, because the condensate is there and gravitates. Reading the
unrelaxed state as reduced capture, $\Gamma<1$ in equation~\eqref{eq:RARmod}, lowers the boost and moves
cluster masses the wrong way; the infall configuration raises them. For the cluster of \S47.5, as
fractions of the hydrostatic mass:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
\toprule
$r$ [kpc] & support ($f=1$) & reduced capture ($\Gamma=0.5$) & infall ($f=0$) & $f=0.2$ \\
\midrule
$250$ & $0.28$ & $0.13$ & $0.83$ & $0.72$ \\
$500$ & $0.38$ & $0.17$ & $0.82$ & $0.73$ \\
$1000$ & $0.55$ & $0.22$ & $0.84$ & $0.78$ \\
$1500$ & $0.68$ & $0.25$ & $0.82$ & $0.80$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The infall configuration---the cosmic share in a halo of concentration $c_{500}=2.6$
($c_{200}\simeq4$)---carries $0.82$--$0.84$ of the hydrostatic mass at every radius; the
remainder is the depletion of cluster baryons below the cosmic fraction ($M_{\rm HSE}/M_b=7.6$ at
$r_{500}=1.29$ Mpc, against $6.35$ for the cosmic share), which a collapsed halo shares.

\emph{Dynamical age.} \sD\ Relaxation proceeds on the system's dynamical time. A disk of radius
$20$ kpc rotating at $200$ km\,s$^{-1}$ has a crossing time of $0.10$ Gyr and has lived about $100$
of them since assembly; a cluster crossed at $1000$ km\,s$^{-1}$ over $r_{500}$ has a crossing time of
$1.3$ Gyr and has lived about four. If polarization takes $N_{\rm rel}$ crossing times,
$f=1-e^{-N/N_{\rm rel}}$, and galaxies at support ($f\ge0.95$) and clusters short of it ($f\le0.3$)
both follow for
\begin{equation}
11\lesssim N_{\rm rel}\lesssim34 .
\label{eq:Nrel}
\end{equation}
The cluster--galaxy difference is then a difference in relaxation state, not in the law: galaxies
have reached support and clusters have not.

\emph{The relaxation time.} \sD\ A bound system decouples from the expansion when it forms, and
its clock is fixed then: a system collapsed to $200$ times the critical density has
$GM/R^3=100H^2$, so its crossing time is $t_{\rm cross}=1/10H(t_f)$, a fixed fraction of the Hubble
time at formation. \sD\ The polarization of its condensate is renewed once per exposure stage, and a
stage is one arc of the horizon's $C_3$ cycle (\S22.9), $\tau_1/3$, read at the horizon of formation: the phase
returns to a $C_3$-equivalent configuration each time it advances by one arc, $2\pi/3$, and the bound medium's
polarization, defined relative to the phase, is renewed at that period.
Then
\begin{equation}
t_{\rm rel}=\frac{\tau_1(t_f)}{3}=\frac{4\pi^2}{9\sqrt3}\,\frac{1}{H(t_f)},\qquad
N_{\rm rel}=\frac{t_{\rm rel}}{t_{\rm cross}}=\frac{40\pi^2}{9\sqrt3}=25.3,
\label{eq:trel}
\end{equation}
inside the window~\eqref{eq:Nrel} that galaxies and clusters independently require; a full cycle,
$N_{\rm rel}=76$, would leave galaxies short of support. The relaxation state follows from the
formation epoch alone,
\begin{equation}
f=1-\exp\Big[-\frac{9\sqrt3}{4\pi^2}\,H(t_f)\,(t-t_f)\Big],
\end{equation}
and in the matter era, where $Ht=\tfrac23$, it depends only on $D=t/t_f$, the age of the universe in
units of its age when the system formed:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{f=1-\exp\Big[-\frac{D-1}{x_H}\Big],\qquad x_H=\frac{2\pi^2}{3\sqrt3}=3.80 .}
\label{eq:fD}
\end{equation}
A system relaxes on $3.8$ times the age the universe had when it formed, and $3.8$ is the horizon
load of \S25.7: both are half the $C_3$ period in Hubble units, $\tau_1H/2$. At the resonance budget
($t_0=13.79$ Gyr), with the general form above (the boxed one is its matter-era limit), today:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
$z_f$ & $t_f$ [Gyr] & $D=t_0/t_f$ & $f$ \\
\midrule
$0.3$ & $10.25$ & $1.3$ & $0.11$ \\
$0.5$ & $8.58$ & $1.6$ & $0.17$ \\
$1$ & $5.84$ & $2.4$ & $0.32$ \\
$2$ & $3.27$ & $4.2$ & $0.58$ \\
$3$ & $2.14$ & $6.5$ & $0.77$ \\
$4$ & $1.53$ & $9.0$ & $0.88$ \\
$6$ & $0.93$ & $14.9$ & $0.97$ \\
$10$ & $0.47$ & $29.4$ & $1.00$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Half the condensate is polarized for systems formed at $z_f=1.66$ and $90\%$ for $z_f=4.26$. The cluster
of \S47.5, assembled at $z_f=0.5$, has $f=0.17$, and its condensate carries $0.74$, $0.74$, $0.79$ and $0.80$ of the
hydrostatic mass at $250$, $500$, $1000$ and $1500$ kpc, against $0.28$--$0.68$ at support; a disk
whose halo formed at $z_f=6$ has $f=0.97$. \sD\ The renewal once per arc follows from the rotation: the phase returns to a $C_3$-equivalent
configuration each time it advances by one arc, $2\pi/3$, that is after $\tau_1/3$, and the bound medium's
polarization, defined relative to the phase, is renewed at that period (\S23.1).

\emph{Failed relaxation.} \sD\ A major merger returns newly accreted condensate to the infall
configuration. With mergers a Poisson process of mean interval $t_{\rm dis}$ and relaxation needing
$t_{\rm rel}=N_{\rm rel}t_{\rm dyn}$, the probability that a system is disrupted before it reaches
support---a trap---is the probability of at least one merger in $t_{\rm rel}$,
\begin{equation}
p_{\rm trap}=1-e^{-t_{\rm rel}/t_{\rm dis}}
\label{eq:trap}
\end{equation}
(if relaxation is itself exponential with mean $t_{\rm rel}$, $p_{\rm trap}=t_{\rm rel}/(t_{\rm rel}+t_{\rm dis})$).
Across the window, clusters with $t_{\rm dis}\simeq4$ Gyr have $p_{\rm trap}=0.97$--$1.00$ and never
reach support; disk galaxies with $t_{\rm dis}\simeq10$ Gyr have $p_{\rm trap}=0.10$--$0.28$.

\emph{Relaxed systems are quiet.} \sD\ At fixed baryons the deviation from the support law is
$(1-f)\,G(M_{\rm inf}-M_{\rm pol})/r^2$, so the scatter of a population about that law is proportional
to the spread of $1-f$: it vanishes for systems at support and is largest for systems in transit.

\emph{Terminology.} A market has a support level, the floor where buyers step in, and a resistance
level, the ceiling where sellers step in, and the price moves between them. Here the observed
acceleration has a support level, the polarization equilibrium where the boost is fully developed,
and a resistance level, Newton, where no condensate is bound; a bound system starts from its infall
halo and moves toward support as it relaxes. A trap is a merger before support is reached, and
``stairs up, elevator down'' is the quiet of relaxed systems against the violence of disrupted ones.
The levels are not constants: they emerge from the condensate's state, as a market's levels emerge
from the positions of its participants.

\subsection*{47.7\quad Predictions of the relaxation state}
\sP\ The ordering does not depend on $N_{\rm rel}$:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep1pt
\item The mass discrepancy against the support law is ordered by dynamical age since the last major
merger: largest in clusters and recent mergers, smallest in isolated disks.
\item Early-assembled systems, such as fossil groups, lie closer to the support law than clusters of
the same mass.
\item After a major merger a cluster's condensate is centrally concentrated relative to the support
law and relaxes toward it over $N_{\rm rel}$ crossing times.
\item At fixed mass, higher-redshift clusters lie further from support than low-redshift ones.
\item The scatter about the radial acceleration relation is larger for disturbed and interacting
galaxies than for isolated disks; $10$--$30\%$ of disks are trapped.
\item Systems with no bound condensate---the Solar System, wide binaries---sit at resistance whatever
their age.
\item The relaxation state depends on $D=t/t_f$ alone (equation~\eqref{eq:fD}): a galaxy formed at
$z_f=6$ is $49\%$ polarized when seen at $z=2$, $75\%$ at $z=1$ and $97\%$ today; one formed at
$z_f=3$ is $13\%$ polarized at $z=2$. Young systems at high redshift carry their infall halos.
\end{enumerate}

% =====================================================================
\subsection*{47.8\quad Perturbation-level cosmology}
\sD\ On the scales of the CMB and linear large-scale structure ($k\lesssim0.3\ \mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}$)
the defect sector reduces to $\Lambda$CDM with the GRUT budget. The radial mode, with
$m_\delta\ge2\times10^{-20}$ eV, has Jeans scale $k_J(a_{\rm eq})\simeq9\,(m_\delta/10^{-22}\,{\rm
eV})^{1/2}\ \mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}\ge127\ \mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}$ and half-mode scale
$k_{1/2}\simeq4.5\,(m_\delta/10^{-22}\,{\rm eV})^{4/9}\ \mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}\ge47\ \mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}$
\cite{Hu2000}: it is pressureless there. The vacuum is the space-filling defect, with $w=-1$ and
$V_0=0$ (\S38). The transfer functions of \S26.7 and \S39 are therefore GRUT's own on these
scales, and the Boltzmann fit of \S26.7 is the perturbation-level calculation of the isotropic
sector. What remains is the perturbation theory of the modified-gravity sector, which needs the
completion of \S46.7, and of the anisotropic component of \S39.6.

% =====================================================================
\subsection*{47.9\quad Cluster mergers: the passage of two excited geometries}\label{sec:mergers}
\sD\ \emph{Equations.} Varying the action of \S23.1 with respect to $\lambda$, $\theta$ and the metric gives
\begin{equation}
X=X_0,\qquad \nabla_\mu(\lambda\,\partial^\mu\theta)=0,\qquad
T_{\mu\nu}=2\lambda\,\partial_\mu\theta\,\partial_\nu\theta+g_{\mu\nu}\lambda(X-X_0)\ \xrightarrow{\,X=X_0\,}\ \rho\,u_\mu u_\nu ,
\label{eq:merger-fe}
\end{equation}
with $u_\mu=\partial_\mu\theta/\sqrt{X_0}$ and $\rho=2\lambda X_0$. The phase equation has no baryonic source: stars and gas act on the phase only
through the metric. The flow is pressureless and geodesic, $u^\nu\nabla_\nu u_\mu=-\partial_\mu X/2X_0=0$. In the infall state of \S47.6 the excited
geometry is collisionless dust in the weak field,
\begin{equation}
\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G(\rho_b+\rho_\chi),\qquad
\partial_tf_\chi+\mathbf v\!\cdot\!\nabla f_\chi-\nabla\Phi\!\cdot\!\partial_{\mathbf v}f_\chi=0,\qquad \rho_\chi=\!\int\! f_\chi\,d^3v .
\label{eq:merger-vlasov}
\end{equation}
The stress tensor, the geodesic identity and the template below are verified symbolically in Appendix~H of the companion.

\sD\ \emph{Cross terms.} For an operator $\mathcal N$ acting on a field, the cross term of two sources is
$\mathcal N_\times[\phi_1,\phi_2]=\mathcal N[\phi_1+\phi_2]-\mathcal N[\phi_1]-\mathcal N[\phi_2]+\mathcal N[0]$. It vanishes for a linear $\mathcal N$
and is $2\lambda\phi_1\phi_2$ for $\mathcal N[\phi]=\lambda\phi^2$. In the infall state $\mathcal N=\nabla^2$ is linear, so $\Phi=\Phi_A+\Phi_B$ exactly
and the only coupling of two clusters is superposition: $\kappa=\kappa_b+\kappa_A+\kappa_B$, and the contribution of $B$ at the galaxy peak of $A$ is
$\kappa_B(\mathbf x_A)$, the tail of $B$'s profile. It is ordinary gravity and carries no new term. In the support state the law of \S22.9 is nonlinear;
in its deep limit
\begin{equation}
\mathcal N_\times=\frac1{a_c}\nabla\!\cdot\!\Big[\,|\nabla\Phi_A+\nabla\Phi_B|(\nabla\Phi_A+\nabla\Phi_B)-|\nabla\Phi_A|\nabla\Phi_A-|\nabla\Phi_B|\nabla\Phi_B\Big]\neq0,
\label{eq:merger-cross}
\end{equation}
which for collinear fields is $(|\nabla\Phi_A|\nabla\Phi_B+|\nabla\Phi_B|\nabla\Phi_A)/a_c$. This term enters a merger only through the clock below.

\sI\ \emph{Conditional relaxation closure; clock reset at core passage.} Within the model, a major merger is assumed to return the condensate to the infall configuration (the trap, equation~\eqref{eq:trap}); the
clock of equations~\eqref{eq:trel} and~\eqref{eq:fD} is then reset at core passage. Given the first-order closure $\dot f=(1-f)/t_{\rm rel}$ with $f(0)=0$, the share relaxed onto the baryons present is
$f(\Delta t)=1-\exp[-(9\sqrt3/4\pi^2)H(z)\Delta t]$, with $t_{\rm rel}=4\pi^2/(9\sqrt3H)$. At the merger epochs:
\begin{center}\small\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\toprule
system & $z$ & $t_{\rm rel}$ [Gyr] & $f(0.15)$ & $f(0.3)$ & $f(1)$ & $f(2)$ \\ \midrule
1E\,0657$-$56 & 0.296 & 31.4 & 0.0048 & 0.0095 & 0.031 & 0.062 \\
MACS\,J0025.4$-$1222 & 0.586 & 26.4 & 0.0057 & 0.011 & 0.037 & 0.073 \\
Abell\,2744 & 0.308 & 31.2 & 0.0048 & 0.0096 & 0.032 & 0.062 \\
Abell\,520 & 0.199 & 33.2 & 0.0045 & 0.0090 & 0.030 & 0.059 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
At $z=0.296$, $t_{\rm rel}=31.4$ Gyr implies $f(0.15\,\mathrm{Gyr})=0.0048$ and $f(0.20\,\mathrm{Gyr})\simeq0.0064$; $f(1\,\mathrm{Gyr})=0.031$ and $f(2\,\mathrm{Gyr})=0.062$. A passage lasts $\simeq0.2$ Gyr (720\,kpc at $2.6$--$4.7\times10^3$ km\,s$^{-1}$), so $t_{\rm rel}/t_{\rm passage}\gtrsim1.2\times10^2$ and the model's relaxation fraction changes only slightly during the encounter. The value $0.0048$ corresponds to $0.15$ Gyr, not $0.20$ Gyr. Capture is complete for a cluster: $\Gamma=1$ for $\sigma\lesssim1$ km\,s$^{-1}$, and $\Gamma=0.973$ at $v_{\rm esc}=1500$ km\,s$^{-1}$ even
at the web's $\sigma_{1D}=494$ km\,s$^{-1}$ of \S53.9. The clock depends on the redshift of the merger and not on a single value: one $t_{\rm rel}$ does not serve all clusters.

\sH\ \emph{The model lensing map and baryon-share assignment.}
\begin{equation}
\kappa(\mathbf x)=\kappa_b(\mathbf x)+(1-f)\big[\kappa_A(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_A)+\kappa_B(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_B)\big]+f\,\kappa_{\rm pol}(\mathbf x),\qquad
M_{\chi,i}=\frac{\Omega_c}{\Omega_b}\,M^{\rm pre}_{b,i},
\label{eq:merger-map}
\end{equation}
where $\kappa_b$ holds the stars and gas, $\kappa_i$ is the excited geometry cluster $i$ gathered before the merger, from all its baryons including the gas it has
since lost, and $\kappa_{\rm pol}$ is the support-state map of \S22.9 applied to the baryons present. Each excited geometry moves on the geodesics of its own galaxies,
so the peaks sit on the galaxies, the stripped gas carries none, and the lensing masses of the two peaks stand in the ratio of the pre-merger baryon masses, each
$1+\Omega_c/\Omega_b=6.35$ times its baryons (equation~\eqref{eq:cluster-share}). The excess drifts toward the baryons by $f(\Delta t)$ of their separation. Differentiating the same law gives the centroid drift $\mathbf v_{\rm rel}=e^{-\Delta t/t_{\rm rel}}\mathbf D/t_{\rm rel}$ for approximately fixed separation; the derivation and mass-conserving flux are given in Section~\ref{sec:chirp-recoil}. This is a redistribution velocity, not a force-driven acceleration of the geodesic component. The displacement is $0.5\%$ for the Bullet Cluster, at most $3.1\%$ after 1 Gyr and $6\%$ after 2 Gyr.

\sD\ \emph{Computed, on synthetic data.} A particle-mesh evolution of a $10{:}1$ pair, with collisionless galaxies and excited geometry and a two-fluid drag for the gas,
conserves momentum to $2\times10^{-4}$ km\,s$^{-1}$ and energy to $0.2\%$, reaches $3.3\times10^3$ km\,s$^{-1}$ at core passage and, $0.15$ Gyr after it, has the galaxies
$449$ kpc apart, the excess within $20$ and $13$ kpc of its galaxies (a resolution limit: $7$ and $11$ kpc on the finer mesh), and the subcluster's gas $340$--$550$ kpc behind
them for drag strengths $5$--$45$; the excess observables do not move with the drag. The time step is converged (peak convergence changes by $0.02\%$ over a factor of four).
The mesh is not: the peak rises by $9.6\%$ from 64 to 96 cells, and the subcluster expands by $40\%$ in isolation over 1.5 Gyr, so offsets below about $30$ kpc are not resolved.
The ordinary cross-cluster contribution to the excess convergence is $3.4\%$ at the main galaxies and $50\%$ at the subcluster's (the main halo's wing there).

\sD\ \emph{What a merger tests, on synthetic data.} Maps of $24\times24$ pixels ($125$ kpc, smoothing $100$ kpc) with correlated shape, large-scale-structure and model
covariance, with calibration, zero-point and centroid nuisance parameters marginalised (centroids by linearisation, checked against Monte Carlo: $\ln B=48.9$ against $49.2$),
were drawn from the simulated snapshot with the clock applied. With $T=2\ln B$ and covariance-preserving nulls of $2\times10^4$ draws: collisionless against baryon-following
$T=97.7$ (null median $-53.6$, $p_{\rm MC}<5\times10^{-5}$); own against swapped assignment ($A$'s excess on $B$'s galaxies) $T=99.1$, $p_{\rm MC}<5\times10^{-5}$;
GRUT against particle dark matter $T=-0.08$, $p_{\rm MC}=0.86$, power $0.053$ at $5\%$. The Bullet Cluster therefore separates both GRUT and particle dark matter from a
gravity law computed on the baryons present, and does not separate them from each other. The settled share is bounded $f<0.14$ ($95\%$, uniform prior) against GRUT's $0.0048$.
The centroid posteriors are calibrated (400 mocks: $z$ standard deviations $0.99$--$1.06$, $68\%$ coverage $0.66$--$0.70$). Ignoring the pixel correlations overstates the
median $T$ by a factor $4.6$. A Gaussian profile for the excess fits the simulated map acceptably ($\Delta{\rm AIC}=0.0$, whitened residuals consistent with noise) and is not
established: the exponential profile ties it ($0.01$), and in mocks the Gaussian is chosen in $87\%$ of cases when true and in $13$--$37\%$ when the truth is Lorentzian or exponential.

\sP\ (i) No offset between the excess and the galaxies beyond $f(\Delta t)D$, with $D$ the gas--galaxy separation ($\sigma/m=0$). (ii) No galaxy-free peak on the gas holding
more than $f(\Delta t)\le0.06$ of the excess. On the synthetic maps a peak of $q\simeq0.10$ of the excess is the $2\sigma$ detection limit, so a robust one at that level would falsify
this. Abell~520 is therefore a test, not a prediction: the dark core of \cite{Mahdavi2007,Jee2012A520} is absent at comparable significance in \cite{Clowe2012A520,Peel2017}.
(iii) The merger speeds are those of $\Lambda$CDM: the shock at $4740$ km\,s$^{-1}$ and the subcluster at $2600$--$3980$ km\,s$^{-1}$ \cite{SpringelFarrar2007,Milosavljevic2007}
carry the same rarity \cite{ThompsonNagamine2012}, and GRUT gives no relief. Shock heating is standard physics and the same in $\Lambda$CDM. Register rows 89--91.

\sH\ \emph{Physical scope of the merger analogy.} The observed separation of lensing-inferred mass from X-ray gas is consistent with a collisionless gravitating component under the standard lensing/GR interpretation; it does not itself identify that component as GRUT's phase sector. The merger relaxation map below is a conditional model closure, not an identity of gravitational lensing.

\sS\ \emph{Three phases and observational classification.} The infall phase precedes first core passage; the core-passage phase contains shock-heated, decelerated intracluster plasma and more nearly collisionless galaxy/lensing-mass trajectories; the post-passage phase is dynamically disturbed and evolves through shocks, orbital motion and relaxation. The condition $f=0$ is the initial condition at the post-passage clock reset, $\Delta t=0$, not a universal statement that every pre-merger system has zero relaxation. Bullet Cluster and MACS J0025.4$-$1222 show gas--lensing-mass separation (\href{https://science.nasa.gov/image-detail/hubble-chandra-bulletcluster-stsci-01evvebdtedwkk5j4k58a3j734/}{NASA Bullet Cluster overview}; \href{https://science.nasa.gov/missions/chandra/a-clash-of-clusters/}{NASA MACS J0025 overview}). El Gordo is usually reconstructed as an ongoing system shortly after first core passage, with outgoing/returning alternatives (\href{https://doi.org/10.1093/mnras/stv1713}{MNRAS dynamical reconstruction}); Pandora's Cluster/Abell 2744 is a complex multi-subcluster collision, not a clean relaxed endpoint (\href{https://science.nasa.gov/asset/hubble/pandoras-cluster-abell-2744/}{NASA overview}). These are distinct clusters, not successive snapshots of one merger; projection and phase degeneracies are discussed in the \href{https://doi.org/10.3390/universe12080249}{2026 review of cluster mergers}.

The local merger is used here as an analogy for the separation of collisional and collisionless components. It does not, by itself, derive a cosmic conformal junction, a sign flip of the scale factor, or information transfer between aeons. In particular, using the same dimensionless $t_{\rm rel}H$ at cluster and cosmological scales is a framework identification; its microphysical validity is not established by the geodesic equation. Testing is deferred in this revision.

% =====================================================================
\section{Limits and the Finite Clock}\label{sec:clock}
% =====================================================================
Every dimensionful quantity in GRUT is a pure number times a monomial in $c$, $\hbar$ and $r$ and a
power of $N$ (\S44.6). The speed of light is therefore not the only limit: the horizon bounds every
quantity above and the cell bounds it below, and the bounds move with the horizon. This section
sorts the bounds into those that are constant, those that freeze and those that never stop moving,
and measures where we are in time.

\subsection*{48.1\quad Three kinds of bound}
\sD\ (i) \emph{Constant limits} contain no horizon and no particular particle or event: $c$; the
total force $F=c^4/G$; the largest force one body can exert on another, $c^4/4G$, reached by two black
holes in contact; and the maximum power $c^5/4G$ (\S43B.6). They are the same at every epoch.
(ii) \emph{Ceilings and floors} contain the horizon. The ceilings are the length $r$, the time $r/c$,
the mass $\MH=c^2r/2G$, the energy $\MH c^2$ and the momentum $\MH c$. The floors are $\ell_P$ and
$t_P$; the mass whose Compton size is the horizon, $\hbar/cr$, below which nothing can be localized
inside the universe; the energy $\hbar c/r$ and momentum $\hbar/r$ of one quantum across the horizon;
and the force $\hbar c/r^2$, the smallest momentum delivered over the longest time. Each ceiling over
its floor is $\sqrt{N/2\pi}$ for length, time and acceleration, $N/4\pi$ for mass, energy, momentum
and weight, and $N/2\pi$ for force: the finite cell count is the limit behind every limit. (iii)
\emph{Clock readings} are built from a particular event. A quantity that contains the age at
recombination or at first starlight falls as $1/t$ and is not a limit: the rest energy of an electron
spread over $t_0/t_{\rm rec}=37\,100$ Planck lengths, $1.37\times10^{17}$ N today, reads the clock.

\subsection*{48.2\quad The freeze}
\sD\ The cell count is $N=2\pi r^2/\ell_P^2$ with $r=c/H$, so $N\propto H^{-2}$. With the vacuum of
\S26.4, $w=-1$, the Hubble rate tends to $H_\infty=H_0\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda}$, and for a flat universe
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{N(t)}{N_\infty}=\frac{H_\infty^2}{H(t)^2}=\Omega_\Lambda(t)}
\label{eq:freeze}
\end{equation}
exactly: the fraction of its final cells the universe holds is its dark-energy fraction. Today it is
$0.684$, with $N_0=4.54\times10^{122}$ and $N_\infty=N_0/\Omega_\Lambda=6.63\times10^{122}$; the horizon
stops at $r_\infty=r/\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda}=17.56$ Gly, against $14.52$ Gly now. The ceilings and floors
freeze with it, reaching $90\%$, $99\%$ and $99.9\%$ of their final cell count at $21.3$, $35.0$ and
$48.5$ Gyr (resonance budget):
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
\toprule
quantity & bound & today & final \\
\midrule
length & $r$ (ceiling) & $1.37\times10^{26}\ \mathrm{m}$ & $1.66\times10^{26}\ \mathrm{m}$ \\
time & $r/c$ (ceiling) & $4.58\times10^{17}\ \mathrm{s}$ & $5.54\times10^{17}\ \mathrm{s}$ \\
mass & $\MH$ (ceiling) & $9.25\times10^{52}\ \mathrm{kg}$ & $1.12\times10^{53}\ \mathrm{kg}$ \\
mass & $\hbar/cr$ (floor) & $2.56\times10^{-69}\ \mathrm{kg}$ & $2.12\times10^{-69}\ \mathrm{kg}$ \\
momentum & $\hbar/r$ (floor) & $7.68\times10^{-61}\ \mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$ & $6.35\times10^{-61}\ \mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$ \\
force & $\hbar c/r^2$ (floor) & $1.68\times10^{-78}\ \mathrm{N}$ & $1.15\times10^{-78}\ \mathrm{N}$ \\
force & $c^4/G$ (constant) & $1.21\times10^{44}\ \mathrm{N}$ & $1.21\times10^{44}\ \mathrm{N}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection*{48.3\quad The maximum-power point}
\sD\ The horizon energy $\EH=c^5/2GH$ grows at $\dot E_H=(c^5/2G)(1+q)$, which equals the maximum power
$c^5/4G$ when the deceleration parameter is $q=-\tfrac12$. With $q=\Omega_r+\tfrac12\Omega_m-\Omega_\Lambda$
and flatness, this is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{N}{N_\infty}=\Omega_\Lambda=\frac23+\frac{\Omega_r}{3}:}
\label{eq:maxpower-point}
\end{equation}
the horizon grows at the maximum power when the universe holds two thirds of its final cells. With
the resonance budget that point was at $z=0.027$, $0.38$ Gyr ago, and we are past it:
$\Omega_\Lambda=0.684>\tfrac23$, $q_0=-0.526$. Before it the horizon grew faster than the maximum power
allows any single system to radiate; after it, slower, falling to zero at the freeze.

\subsection*{48.4\quad The finite aeon}
\sD\ Proper time runs without end, but conformal time, $\eta=\int c\,dt/a$, converges in the future
because the vacuum keeps the expansion accelerating, and it is the clock the reset carries into the
next aeon (\S45). The aeon's conformal length is
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\rm tot}=4.327\,\frac{c}{H_0}=62.8\ \mathrm{Gly},
\end{equation}
of which $46.1$ Gly has passed: \sP\ we are $73.4\%$ of the way through the aeon. The remaining
$16.7$ Gly is the event horizon, the farthest comoving distance a signal sent today can ever reach.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
moment & age & $N/N_\infty=\Omega_\Lambda(t)$ & fraction of the aeon's conformal time \\
\midrule
recombination, $z=1090$ & 371 kyr & $1.27\times10^{-9}$ & $0.015$ \\
first stars, $z=20$ & 178 Myr & $2.32\times10^{-4}$ & $0.166$ \\
$z=2$ & 3.27 Gyr & $0.074$ & $0.458$ \\
$z=0.5$ & 8.58 Gyr & $0.391$ & $0.633$ \\
maximum-power point & 13.41 Gyr & $0.667$ & $0.728$ \\
today & 13.79 Gyr & $0.684$ & $0.734$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The conformal fraction depends on $\Omega_m$ and $\Omega_r$ alone, not on $H_0$, and falls by $0.35$ per
unit of $\Omega_m$.

\subsection*{48.5\quad How this section fails}
\sP\ Each statement has a stated failure:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep1pt
\item The freeze requires $w=-1$ exactly. A dark energy that thaws toward the future sends $H\to0$ and
$N\to\infty$, so there is no final cell count; a phantom one shrinks the horizon. Evolving dark energy
established at $5\sigma$ falsifies \S48.2--48.4 together with \S26.4; the present preference is
$2.7\sigma$ with the CMB and $3.2\sigma$ with supernovae (register row~6).
\item The identity~\eqref{eq:freeze} requires flatness: $|\Omega_K|>0.003$ at $3\sigma$ falsifies it.
\item The maximum-power point lies in the past only if $q_0<-\tfrac12$: a flat, $w=-1$ fit returning
$q_0>-\tfrac12$ at $3\sigma$ falsifies \S48.3 as a statement about today. GRUT gives $-0.526$ and Planck's
budget $-0.527$.
\item The finite aeon requires the expansion to keep accelerating: an asymptotic equation of state
above $-\tfrac13$ makes the conformal time diverge.
\item The constant limits: a force between two bodies above $c^4/4G$, or a luminosity above
$c^5/4G=9.1\times10^{51}$ W, falsifies them; the largest measured, GW150914, peaked at $0.4\%$ of it.
\item These numbers are not independent tests. $\Omega_\Lambda(t)$, the freeze, the maximum-power epoch
and the conformal fraction are functions of $\Omega_m$ (and $H_0$ for the dimensional ones), and are
tested through the $\Omega_m$ and $H_0$ rows of Appendix~\ref{app:variance}. With the CMB-independent DESI
Lyman-$\alpha$ measurement, $\Omega_m=0.325\pm0.018$ \cite{DESI2026lya}, the conformal fraction is
$0.7310\pm0.0062$, $+0.2\sigma$ from GRUT's $0.7321$; Planck's $\Omega_m$, inferred within $\Lambda$CDM,
gives $0.7344\pm0.0025$ ($-0.9\sigma$).
\item A drift of the gravitational constant at $2(1+q)H$ would mean fixed, stretching cells, and
reverses \S48.6.
\item A measured drift of $\alpha$ that tracks the age reverses \S48.7.
\item The first-starlight hypothesis of \S48.8 fails if 21-cm coupling is detected above $z=30.1$,
below $45.5$ MHz---starlight before $t_\star$---or if the onset is measured well after it: first
starlight at $z\approx20$, $1/78$ of the age, seen at $67.6$ MHz, excludes it. It also fails if GRUT's
condensate cannot form stars by $z_\star$ at any allowed mass (\S48.9). With the rotation's birefringence it fails if the onset and the
CMB's twist miss~\eqref{eq:dawn-twist} by more than $5\sigma$ together (\S48.8).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{48.6\quad The cells do not stretch}
\sD\ Space expands by gaining cells, not by stretching them, and the data decide which. With
$N=2\pi r^2/\ell_P^2$, GRUT's gravitational constant is $G=2\pi c^3r^2/\hbar N$
(Section~\ref{sec:G}). If the cell count were fixed while the horizon grew, each cell would stretch
with it, and today
\begin{equation}
\frac{\dot G}{G}=2\,\frac{\dot r}{r}=2(1+q_0)H_0=6.6\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}} .
\end{equation}
Lunar laser ranging finds $\dot G/G=(7.1\pm7.6)\times10^{-14}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ \cite{HofmannMuller2018},
three orders of magnitude smaller. Holding $G$ fixed and letting $\hbar$ carry the change instead
would move the fine-structure constant as $r^{-2}$, by a factor $E(2)^2=9.4$ between redshift $2$ and
today, against quasar spectra that hold $\Delta\alpha/\alpha$ to about a part per million
\cite{Murphy2022}. \sP\ The Planck length is therefore fixed and the cell count grows as the square
of the horizon, $N(t)=2\pi r(t)^2/\ell_P^2$, which is what \S48.2 uses.

\subsection*{48.7\quad The fine-structure constant sets when light escapes}
\sD\ If $\alpha$ read the clock, $\alpha\propto1/t$, it would change today at
$\dot\alpha/\alpha=-1/t_0=-7.3\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}}$, be $4.2$ times larger in the light of
quasars at $z=2$, and $3.7\times10^4$ times larger at recombination, where no atom could form. Optical
clocks hold $\dot\alpha/\alpha=(1.0\pm1.1)\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ \cite{Lange2021}, and quasar
spectra hold $\Delta\alpha/\alpha$ to about a part per million \cite{Murphy2022}: $\alpha$ belongs with
the constant limits of \S48.1.

\sD\ It is, however, the constant that sets when light escapes. The universe becomes transparent
when the photon bath can no longer ionize hydrogen, whose binding energy is
$B=\tfrac12\alpha^2m_ec^2=13.6$ eV. The Saha balance,
\begin{equation}
\frac{x_e^2}{1-x_e}=\frac{1}{\eta}\,\frac{\pi^2}{2\zeta(3)}\Big(\frac{m_ec^2}{2\pi kT}\Big)^{3/2}e^{-B/kT},
\end{equation}
fixes the temperature at which half the hydrogen is neutral, and with $1+z=T/T_0$ and the budget's
expansion history
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\ln(1+z_{\rm rec})}{d\ln\alpha}=2.07,\qquad
\frac{d\ln D_{\rm rec}}{d\ln\alpha}=3.54,\qquad D_{\rm rec}\equiv\frac{t_0}{t_{\rm rec}} .
\label{eq:alpha-escape}
\end{equation}
The first exponent lies slightly above $2$ because of the logarithm in the Saha balance; the second
lies between $3$ (matter) and $4$ (radiation) because the two share the expansion at $z\simeq1100$.
The ratio of the age of the universe to the moment light escaped, $3.7\times10^4$ at $z=1090$, therefore
carries $\alpha^{3.5}$: a universe with a different $\alpha$ lets its light out at a different fraction of
its age. $\alpha$ is the clock-setter, not the clock.

\subsection*{48.8\quad First starlight}
\sH\ With $\alpha$ constant and the first starlight a fixed moment $t_\star$, the ratio $t_\star/t$
passes through $\alpha$ once. The hypothesis is that it does so now,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{t_\star=\alpha\,t_0,\qquad t_0=137.036\,t_\star ,}
\label{eq:first-light}
\end{equation}
a selection of the present epoch (\S36.6) by the moment stars first shone, as \S48.3 places us just
past the maximum-power point. \sP\ At the resonance budget it predicts first starlight at
\begin{equation}
t_\star=100.6\ \mathrm{Myr},\qquad z_\star=29.6,
\end{equation}
and the onset of the 21-cm signal it drives,
when starlight first couples the hydrogen spin temperature to the gas, at
\begin{equation}
\nu_\star=\frac{1420.4\ \mathrm{MHz}}{1+z_\star}=46.4\ \mathrm{MHz}.
\end{equation}
It is consistent with what is seen. The earliest spectroscopically confirmed galaxy, at $z=14.32$
\cite{Carniani2024}, is seen at $286$ Myr, $1/48.3$ of the age; the absorption EDGES reported at $78$ MHz
($z\simeq17.2$, $220$ Myr) \cite{Bowman2018}, which SARAS~3 does not confirm \cite{Singh2022}, would
require starlight earlier still. HERA, REACH, SKA-Low and radio arrays on the lunar far side are built
to find the onset. If $t_\star$ is measured, equation~\eqref{eq:first-light} gives the age of the
universe independently of the distance ladder.

\emph{The two ends.} \sD\ The 21-cm photons leave the first-starlight end of the history and arrive at
ours. Three unit-free ratios connect the ends:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
clock & first starlight & today & link between the ends \\
\midrule
cosmic time & $100.6$ Myr & $13.79$ Gyr & $t_\star/t_0=\alpha$ (the hypothesis) \\
scale factor & wavelength $0.211$ m & $6.46$ m & $a_\star/a_0=1/30.6$ \\
conformal time & $13.5\%$ of the aeon & $73.4\%$ & $59.9\%$ of the aeon between them \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The shell of first starlight lies at a comoving distance of $3.56\times10^{26}$ m ($37.6$ Gly), which
sets the angular scales a 21-cm map of the onset resolves. The three links are not independent: all
follow from equation~\eqref{eq:first-light} and the budget, so one measurement of the onset tests them
together and counts once. A length at one end set against a clock at the other---the $6.46$ m
wavelength against the $4.36\times10^{26}$ m conformal radius---carries units and no physics. \sD\ GRUT must itself form stars by $z_\star$. In the rotation
reading the cosmic share is dust with zero sound speed (\S23.1): it suppresses no halos, minihalos form as
in cold dark matter, and the first stars of the observable universe form near $z\simeq65$ in the rarest
peaks \cite{Naoz2006}, well before $z_\star=29.6$. The bound on $m_\delta$ that a condensate would impose does not
arise.

\emph{Between physical epochs.} \sH\ With the present selected by the resonance (H3, \S25.11), first starlight no longer
selects it. The hypothesis becomes a relation between two physical epochs, cosmic dawn and the resonance at which the horizon's wobble
falls into step with the spin, and needs no observer:
\begin{equation}
t_\star=\alpha\,t_{\rm res},\qquad H_\infty t_\star=\alpha\,\theta_0=5.73\times10^{-3},
\label{eq:dawn-res}
\end{equation}
with $\theta_0=H_\infty t_{\rm res}=0.78545$ the phase turned by the spin since the beginning (\S36.13). \sP\ At the resonance budget
$t_\star=100.6$ Myr, $z_\star=29.6$ and $\nu_\star=46.4$ MHz; a $\pm3\%$ timing places the onset at $z=29.0$--$30.2$, $45.5$--$47.3$ MHz.
Stated at the maximum-power epoch instead (\S48.10), $t_\star=\alpha t_{\rm mp}=97.9$ Myr, $z=30.2$, $45.6$ MHz, $2.7\%$ earlier. \sM\ No
starlight before $t_\star$ is known. The earliest confirmed galaxy is at $z=14.44$, $280$ Myr \cite{Naidu2026}; the claimed $z\simeq32$
candidate ``Capotauro'', which would have shone at about $90$ Myr, moves on the sky and is a Y dwarf $730\pm110$ pc away \cite{Liu2026}.
\sO\ The hypothesis remains a hypothesis: the onset of cosmic dawn depends on the amplitude of the primordial spectrum and the physics of
the first stars, which the framework does not compute (\S45.10), so~\eqref{eq:dawn-res} is not derived. It is tested by the 21-cm onset,
register row~76.

\emph{In cycles} \sD\ A frequency is a number of cycles per second, and the onset is read as one: a radiometer
counts the cycles of the 21-cm wave as they arrive, while each hydrogen atom emitted $\nu_{21}=1420.405751768$ MHz, so the observable is a
ratio of counts, the cycles received per cycle emitted, $\nu_\star/\nu_{21}=a_\star=1/(1+z_\star)$, in which the second cancels. The spin's
phase is a count of the same kind. In a flat universe of matter and vacuum $\sinh(\tfrac32H_\infty t)=\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda/\Omega_m}\,a^{3/2}$,
so with~\eqref{eq:dawn-res} and the present at the resonance
\begin{equation}
\frac{\nu_\star}{\nu_{21}}=\Big[\frac{\sqrt{4\pi^2-27}}{3\sqrt3}\,\sinh\Big(\alpha\,\mathrm{artanh}\,\frac{3\sqrt3}{2\pi}\Big)\Big]^{2/3}=\frac{1}{30.81},
\label{eq:dawn-cycles}
\end{equation}
$46.10$ MHz, and $1/30.61$, $46.41$ MHz, with radiation: the fine-structure constant and the $C_3$ ratio, as in the
birefringence~\eqref{eq:beta-closed}, with no clock, and with a Hubble constant only in the radiation's $0.7\%$.

\sD\ The birefringence counts turns of the same weighted phase. The polarization turns by $\kappa\alpha/2\pi$ for each radian the spin
turns while the light is in flight (\S49.12), $\beta=(\kappa\alpha/2\pi)\,H_\infty(t_0-t_{\rm rec})$, and~\eqref{eq:dawn-res} places cosmic dawn
where the spin had turned $\alpha$ of its present phase, $H_\infty t_\star=\alpha H_\infty t_0$. The fine-structure constant cancels between
them, and so does the present's phase:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H_\infty t_\star=\frac{2\pi}{\kappa}\,\beta=\frac{\pi}{3}\,\beta,\qquad
\frac{\nu_\star}{\nu_{21}}=\Big[\sqrt{\frac{\Omega_m}{\Omega_\Lambda}}\,\sinh\frac{\pi\beta}{2}\Big]^{2/3},}
\label{eq:dawn-twist}
\end{equation}
to $3\times10^{-5}$, the light's departure at recombination, with $\beta$ taken by magnitude. The spin's phase at cosmic dawn is the CMB's
twist over the anomaly's weight per radian, $\kappa/2\pi=3/\pi$. Two counts of cycles are tied with no $\alpha$, no clock and no Hubble
constant: the twist of the oldest light, a fraction of a turn, and the onset of the 21-cm signal, a ratio of received to emitted cycles.
$\Omega_m/\Omega_\Lambda$ enters only as the shape of the expansion, which the resonance fixes; Planck's $\Omega_m$ moves the onset by $0.1\%$.

\sP\ \sM\ With the measured angle, $0.277^\circ\pm0.057^\circ$ \cite{Eskilt2026}, \eqref{eq:dawn-twist} places the onset at
$42.7^{+5.6}_{-6.0}$ MHz, $z_\star=32.2$, $0.6\sigma$ below~\eqref{eq:dawn-cycles}, as far as the angle lies from its closed form. The
onset moves as $\beta^{0.66}$: a twist known to $\pm0.02^\circ$ places it to $\pm2.0$ MHz, and one known to $\pm0.01^\circ$ to $\pm1.0$ MHz.
Read the other way, first starlight at $z\approx20$, $67.6$ MHz (\S48.5), would need $\beta=0.55^\circ$, $4.8\sigma$ above the measured
angle. An onset and a twist that miss~\eqref{eq:dawn-twist} by more than $5\sigma$ together exclude the hypothesis with the rotation's
birefringence, and a twist settled at zero, which would place cosmic dawn at the beginning, excludes it outright. \sS\ Correlations
between 21-cm fluctuations and the $B$ modes a birefringent scalar induces have been studied \cite{Kadota2019}; a relation between the
CMB's twist and the epoch of cosmic dawn has, to our knowledge, not been proposed. \sO\ The relation tests~\eqref{eq:dawn-res} against a
measurement already in hand but does not derive it: it states the hypothesis between two counts of the one spin, with $\alpha$ the common
weight. Register row~78.




\subsection*{48.9\quad What the hypothesis tests, changes and does not do}
\emph{Definition.} \sD\ The first starlight $t_\star$ is the onset of cosmic dawn: the moment the global
Lyman-$\alpha$ background from stars first couples the hydrogen spin temperature to the gas, seen in the
sky-averaged 21-cm signal. It is not the first individual star: in the rarest density peaks of the
observable volume a first star forms far earlier, near $z\approx65$ \cite{Naoz2006}.

\emph{The test.} \sP\ Timing the onset to $\pm3\%$ places it at $z=29.0$--$30.2$, $45.5$--$47.3$ MHz; to
$\pm10\%$, at $z=27.7$--$31.8$, $43.3$--$49.5$ MHz. The falsifier is one-sided and sharp: coupling
detected below $45.5$ MHz means starlight before $t_\star$ and excludes the hypothesis. A later onset
disfavors it more softly, because coupling follows the first stars with a lag. Instruments reaching
below $50$ MHz---LOFAR's low band, NenuFAR, LEDA, MIST and radio arrays on the lunar far side---see the
onset itself; SKA-Low, HERA, REACH and EDGES begin near $50$ MHz ($z=27.4$) and test what follows it.

\emph{What it changes in GRUT.} \sD\ The first stars form in minihalos of $10^5$--$10^6M_\odot$, and
the condensate suppresses halos below its half-mode mass,
$M_{1/2}=\tfrac{4\pi}{3}\bar\rho_m(\pi/k_{1/2})^3$ with $k_{1/2}\simeq4.5\,(m_\delta/10^{-22}\,\mathrm{eV})^{4/9}\
\mathrm{Mpc^{-1}}$ \cite{Hu2000}: $4.9\times10^7M_\odot$ at the Lyman-$\alpha$ bound $m_\delta=2\times10^{-20}$ eV.
Forming first stars by $z=29.6$ therefore requires $M_{1/2}\lesssim10^6M_\odot$, that is
\begin{equation}
m_\delta\gtrsim4\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{eV}\qquad(2\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{eV\ for\ }10^5M_\odot\ \mathrm{minihalos}),
\end{equation}
twenty times the Lyman-$\alpha$ bound. At that mass the Jeans length is $20$--$64$ pc and galaxy cores
shrink accordingly; the Solar System stays far inside it (\S46.3). The estimate uses the half-mode
criterion and is good to an order of magnitude.

\emph{What it could settle.} \sP\ The age of the universe, independently of the distance ladder and of
the CMB's inference of $H_0$: $t_0=137.036\,t_\star$. A $3\%$ timing of the onset gives $H_0$ to $3\%$,
$\pm2.0\ \mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$, and separates the Planck and SH0ES values, which differ by
$8.4\%$, at about $3\sigma$. It also fixes the present epoch, $t_0=t_\star/\alpha$, as a large-number
relation of the kind Dirac noted and Dicke read as a condition on when observers can exist
\cite{Dicke1961}; stated at the maximum-power point instead of the present (\S48.10), it needs no
observer. Counted in cycles the onset needs no clock either: \eqref{eq:dawn-twist} sets it against the CMB's twist,
and a Hubble constant follows from it only through the CMB's physical matter density, which turns the count into years (\S48.8).

\emph{What it does not do.} It proves nothing; it does not derive $\alpha$, since $t_0$ is an epoch rather
than a constant; and the present agreement is not evidence. With first starlight estimated between
$50$ and $200$ Myr, a ratio drawn log-uniformly from that range falls within $35\%$ of $\alpha$ with
probability $0.53$, within $10\%$ with $0.15$, and within $3\%$ with $0.04$. Stated here in advance, an
onset measured within $3\%$ of $z_\star$ would be a one-in-twenty-five chance if the hypothesis were
false: the level at which the relation stops being plausibly a coincidence.


\subsection*{48.10\quad What dark energy is}
Put together, \S26.4, \S38, \S48.2 and \S48.6 say what the dark energy is.

\sD\ (i) The scalar sector carries no vacuum energy, $V_0=0$ (\S38), so the vacuum of quantum
fields does not gravitate as a cosmological constant here, and the dark energy is the space-filling
defect of the horizon (\S26.4), with $w=-1$. (ii) The cells do not stretch (\S48.6), and with $w=-1$
their number stops at $N_\infty$ (\S48.2). (iii) Since $H_\infty^2=2\pi c^2/N_\infty\ell_P^2$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\rho_\Lambda=\frac34\,\frac{\rho_P}{N_\infty},\qquad \rho_P=\frac{c^7}{\hbar G^2},}
\label{eq:rhoL-capacity}
\end{equation}
exactly: $\rho_\Lambda/\rho_P=1.130\times10^{-123}=3/4N_\infty$ with $N_\infty=6.63\times10^{122}$.
Equivalently, each cell of the final horizon holds one horizon-scale quantum,
$\hbar c/4\pi r_\infty=1.51\times10^{-53}$ J, and together they hold the whole energy of the final
horizon, $1.01\times10^{70}$ J. (iv) The rise of dark energy is the filling of that capacity,
$\Omega_\Lambda(t)=N(t)/N_\infty$. \sP\ Dark energy is the energy of the universe's finite information
capacity: the Planck energy density shared over every cell the horizon will ever hold.

\emph{What it answers.} The cosmological constant problem is that the vacuum energy lies some
$10^{123}$ below the Planck density \cite{Weinberg1989}. Here that number is $3/4N_\infty$: the vacuum
energy is shared over $10^{123}$ cells rather than held by each. The holographic bound on effective
field theory \cite{CKN1999} and holographic dark energy \cite{Li2004} reach the same scaling; GRUT gives
the coefficient, $\tfrac34$, and the reason no larger vacuum energy competes, $V_0=0$. The why-now
problem sharpens as well. The present lies $0.38$ Gyr after the maximum-power point, where
$\Omega_\Lambda=\tfrac23$ (\S48.3), and the first-starlight relation can be stated between two physical
epochs, with no reference to observers:
\begin{equation}
t_\star=\alpha\,t_{\rm mp}=97.9\ \mathrm{Myr},\qquad z_\star=30.2,\qquad \nu_\star=45.6\ \mathrm{MHz},
\end{equation}
$2.7\%$ from $\alpha t_0$ and inside the $\pm3\%$ window of \S48.9; a $1\%$ timing distinguishes the two
readings.

\emph{What it does not.} The magnitude is not derived from geometry: it is relocated to $N_\infty$, the
size of the final horizon in cells, which is the framework's one boundary datum (\S44.6). The question
why $\Lambda$ is small becomes why the universe is $10^{61}$ Planck lengths across. Carried through the
reset (\S45) as the next aeon's information capacity, $N_\infty$ would be a property of the cycle rather
than an accident; that is not yet shown. Equation~\eqref{eq:rhoL-capacity} holds as an identity in any
flat universe with a constant vacuum; its content in GRUT is causal---the vacuum energy is set by the
capacity, because nothing else is left to set it---and it fails with $w=-1$: an evolving dark energy
established at $5\sigma$ (register row~6) means dark energy is not the capacity energy.


\subsection*{48.11\quad The vacuum of every field}
\sD\ The capacity bound holds for every field, not only for the defect scalar. The standard estimate
gives each Planck volume a Planck energy, $\rho_{\rm vac}\simeq\rho_P$ \cite{Weinberg1989}. Inside the
horizon that is an energy $\rho_P\cdot\tfrac43\pi r^3$, while the horizon condition of \S8 fixes the whole
content at $\MH c^2=c^4r/2G$. Their ratio is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\rho_P\cdot\tfrac43\pi r^3}{\MH c^2}=\frac{8\pi}{3}\,\frac{r^2}{\ell_P^2}=\frac{4N}{3},
\label{eq:vac-excess}
\end{equation}
the same $10^{123}$. A vacuum of Planck density would put the horizon's content inside a Schwarzschild
radius $4N/3$ times the horizon: the region would be a black hole $10^{123}$ times its own size. Gravity
couples to the energy of every field alike, so the horizon condition caps the vacuum energy of all
fields together---electrons, photons, quarks and the defect scalar---at the critical density,
$\rho_{\rm vac}\le\rho_c(t)$. This is the holographic bound of \cite{CKN1999}, and here it is the horizon
condition itself. At the freeze the vacuum saturates the cap, and the energy per cell is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\hbar c}{4\pi r_\infty}=\tfrac12k_BT_\infty,\qquad T_\infty=\frac{\hbar H_\infty}{2\pi k_B}=2.18\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm{K}:
\end{equation}
one quadratic degree of freedom at the horizon temperature per cell, equipartition over the horizon's
degrees of freedom \cite{Padmanabhan2010}, at the exchange rate of \S24, $\EH=T_{\rm GH}S$. \sP\ The
discrepancy of $10^{123}$ is therefore a counting error in the standard estimate---a Planck energy per
volume cell in place of half the horizon temperature per surface cell---for all fields together.
\sD\ Mode by mode, the cap is a cutoff. A local field theory in the horizon is consistent only while
no state it describes lies inside its own Schwarzschild radius \cite{CKN1999}. Its zero-point energy,
$\hbar ck_{\max}^4/16\pi^2$, reaches the critical density at
\begin{equation}
k_{\max}=\frac{(6\pi)^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\ell_Pr}},\qquad \frac{2\pi}{k_{\max}}=0.142\ \mathrm{mm},
\end{equation}
so modes shorter than the geometric mean of the Planck length and the horizon---the length of the
dark-energy density, $(\hbar c/\rho_\Lambda c^2)^{1/4}=0.088$ mm---are not independent degrees of freedom and
do not gravitate separately.

\subsection*{48.12\quad The present in Planck cells}
\sH\ (H4) The resonance (H3, \S36.7) places the present in the expansion history; the cell count $N$ places it in Planck units, and
Bridge theorem B4 (\S44.6) leaves $N$ the framework's one boundary datum. H4 fixes it by the electric coupling: the present is the epoch at
which the horizon, measured in Planck lengths, satisfies
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\ln\frac{r}{\lP}=\frac1\alpha+\ln8\pi+\frac{3\pi}{2}\,\alpha ,\qquad \frac{r}{\lP}=8\pi\,e^{1/\alpha+3\pi\alpha/2}.}
\label{eq:present-cells}
\end{equation}
The $8\pi$ is the horizon's solid angle $4\pi$ taken over the double cover of the fold, $S^3\to\mathbb{RP}^3$ (\S1.8--1.9), the factor that
also sets the area quantum $8\pi\lP^2$ (\S36.11); the correction is the three arcs of $C_3$ times the conjugate pair's quarter turn,
$3\times\tfrac\pi2$, at first order in $\alpha$. Written $r=8\pi\lP\,e^{1/\alpha}$, it has the form of dimensional transmutation,
$\Lambda=M\,e^{-2\pi/b\alpha}$, with $b=2\pi$: the horizon is the length at which the electric coupling, counted from one Planck cell, has run
through $1/\alpha$ e-folds.

\sD\ It is a condition on the epoch, not a law for $\alpha$. As a law it would tie $\alpha$ to the growing horizon,
$d(1/\alpha)/dt=-\dot H/H=(1+q)H$: a drift $\dot\alpha/\alpha=-2.4\times10^{-13}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ today against
$(1.0\pm1.1)\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ from atomic clocks \cite{Lange2021}, a shift of $0.4\%$ at $z=1$, and $1/\alpha=127$ at
recombination; on the constant final horizon it would give $137.226$. With $\alpha$ constant the condition holds once, and it selects the
present as H3 and $t_\star=\alpha t_0$ (\S48.8) do. Teller's $1/\alpha\simeq\ln(\hbar c/Gm_p^2)$ was a relation of this kind read as a law of
varying constants \cite{Uzan2003}; H4 is read as a selection.

\sP\ With CODATA's $\alpha$, \eqref{eq:present-cells} gives, with no cosmological input,
\begin{equation}
H_0=\frac{c}{8\pi\lP}\,e^{-1/\alpha-3\pi\alpha/2}=67.3765\pm0.0007\ \mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}},\qquad
N=128\pi^3\,e^{2/\alpha+3\pi\alpha}=4.534\times10^{122},
\label{eq:H0-cells}
\end{equation}
the error being $G$'s. The CMB fit of the resonance budget gives $67.35\pm0.06$ (\S26.7): the two selections of the present, one by the
expansion history and one by the Planck cells, agree at $0.4\sigma$, and together they predict the acoustic angle of the budget,
$100\theta_*=1.04124$, against Planck's $1.04110\pm0.00031$. With H3 the cosmological constant follows in Planck units,
\begin{equation}
\Lambda\lP^2=\frac{81}{256\pi^4}\,e^{-2/\alpha-3\pi\alpha}=2.843\times10^{-122},\qquad
\frac{\rho_\Lambda}{\rho_P}=\frac{3}{4N_\infty}=\frac{81}{2048\pi^5}\,e^{-2/\alpha-3\pi\alpha}=1.131\times10^{-123},
\label{eq:Lambda-alpha}
\end{equation}
so the $10^{-122}$ of the cosmological constant problem is $e^{-2/\alpha}$: the vacuum is as small as the present is old. \sO\ The $8\pi$ and
the $\tfrac{3\pi}2$ were read after the value was known, and other small combinations fall inside the $\pm0.0009$ that the CMB fit's error
allows in $1/\alpha$; a derivation would obtain the coefficient $b=2\pi$ and the correction from the field equations. \sP\ H4 fails if the
converged $H_0$ settles away from $67.38$ at $5\sigma$, if the budget's acoustic angle settles away from $1.04124$ at $5\sigma$, or if
$\alpha$ is found to vary (register row~86).


% =====================================================================
\section{The Local Baryonic Coupling}\label{sec:coupling}
This section follows the rotation of Section~\ref{sec:rotation} into galaxies: the baryons modulate its frequency;
the modulation, held at the rotation's rate, is a medium bound around them (Sections~\ref{sec:complete}
and~\ref{sec:condensation}); and the bound medium gives the flat rotation curves, the Solar-System limit and the
birefringence of the CMB.

\subsection*{49.1\quad Why the global frequency alone cannot hold a galaxy}
If the whole universe rotates at one frequency, the orbital speed in a galaxy depends on where it sits,
$v_0(\chi)=\omega_0R_H\sin\chi$: faster toward one end of the rotation and slower toward the other. That
is not flat, and it is not what is observed.

\subsection*{49.2\quad The baryons modulate the frequency}
The baryons do not create the gravity; they modulate the frequency. They make the phase wind faster or
slower depending on where the matter is:
\begin{equation}
\omega(\chi)=\omega_0+\delta\omega_b(\chi),\qquad
\theta_{\rm phase}=[\omega_0+\delta\omega_b(\chi)]\,t+n\varphi .
\end{equation}
The topology does not change: $\oint d\theta_{\rm phase}=2\pi n$, and $n$ is still an integer. The global
frequency does not change: it is still fixed by the $C_3$ eigenvalue. What changes is the local rate at
which the phase is sampled. Requiring a flat velocity $v_{\rm flat}$ in
$v(\chi)=R_H\sin\chi\,[\omega_0+\delta\omega_b(\chi)]$ gives the response equation
\begin{equation}
\delta\omega_b(\chi)=\frac{v_{\rm flat}}{R_H\sin\chi}-\omega_0,\qquad
\omega(\chi)=\frac{v_{\rm flat}}{R_H\sin\chi},
\end{equation}
a response, not a new winding number. With a response functional of the baryonic density,
$\delta\omega_b=\mathcal C[\rho_b]$, the local frequency is $\omega(\chi)=\omega_0+\mathcal C[\rho_b(\chi)]$.

\subsection*{49.3\quad The flat curve and the transition}
Once the modulation reaches the value that gives the flat velocity, the velocity is flat---not
fitted, not tuned, but what the response equation demands. The transition between the global rotation
and the locally modulated rotation is controlled by a coupling function of the local baryonic
acceleration $g_b=GM_b(<r)/r^2$:
\begin{equation}
\omega(r)=\omega_0+\Gamma_b\!\left(\frac{g_b}{a_c}\right)\left[\frac{v_{\rm flat}(r)}{R_H\sin(r/R_H)}-\omega_0\right],\qquad
v(r)=[1-\Gamma_b]\,\omega_0R_H\sin(r/R_H)+\Gamma_b\,v_{\rm flat}.
\label{eq:rot-law}
\end{equation}
In the outer regions of galaxies, where the acceleration is weak, the coupling is strong and the curve
is flat, $\Gamma_b\to1$. In the inner regions and the Solar System, where the acceleration is strong,
the coupling is suppressed, $\Gamma_b\to0$, and the rotation is the tiny global mode: ordinary
Newtonian behaviour. For a galaxy $r/R_H\sim10^{-5}$, so $\omega(r)\simeq v_{\rm flat}/r$; inside the
baryonic core $r_c$ the response saturates,
$\Gamma_b=\frac{r^2}{r^2+r_c^2}\,\Gamma_{\rm gal}(g_b/a_c)$, so $\omega(0)=\omega_0$ stays finite.

\subsection*{49.4\quad Tests and independent support}
\sP\ The local inertial frame, compared with the quasar frame by high-precision astrometry, tests the
mechanism \cite{Szapudi2021}: with the windings zero (\S23.2), the internal rotation predicts no rotation of
the inertial frame against the quasars. The coupling function, once derived, must
reproduce the rotation curves, satisfy the compact-space constraint, and respect the Solar-System limit,
as one function. Independently, a slowly rotating universe has been shown to be viable: a
G\"odel-inspired rotating model \cite{Godel1949} with present angular velocity
$\simeq2\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{Gyr^{-1}}$, close to the maximal rotation that keeps the tangential speed
below $c$ at the horizon, resolves the Hubble tension \cite{Szigeti2025}. That is a rotation of matter, and
the vorticity bound \cite{Saadeh2016} constrains it; the rotation here is internal and carries no vorticity.

\subsection*{49.5\quad The coupling has two parts}
The baryons tune the phase in two ways at once: through the metric they curve, and through the cone
they carry.

\sD\ \emph{The metric part, from the ground up.} Put the phase into its own field equation on the
metric the baryons curve, $ds^2=-(1+2\Phi/c^2)c^2dt^2+(1-2\Phi/c^2)\,d\mathbf x^2$. For
$\theta=\Omega(\mathbf x)\,t+n\varphi$ the part of $\nabla_\mu(\chi^2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_\nu\theta)=0$ that grows
with time is $t\,\chi^2c\,\nabla^2\Omega$, so $\nabla^2\Omega=0$, and on the closed $S^3$ the only regular
solution is $\Omega=\Omega_0$: a frequency that varied from place to place would wind the phase tighter
every second, and its energy would grow as $t^2$. What the baryons modulate is the frequency a local
clock measures,
\begin{equation}
\Omega_{\rm loc}=\Omega_0\Big(1-\frac{\Phi}{c^2}\Big),\qquad \Gamma_b^{(g)}=-\frac{\Phi}{c^2},
\label{eq:gamma-metric}
\end{equation}
with coefficient exactly one. This is the redshift of the core: the deeper potential slows the local
phase by the same factor that redshifts its light. At the Sun's orbit $|\Phi|/c^2\simeq(1.2$--$1.8)\times10^{-6}$.

\sD\ \emph{The geometric part, by matching.} Requiring the orbit condition $v^2/r=g_b+a_{\rm drag}$ to
reproduce the field equation $\nabla\cdot[\mu\nabla\Phi]=4\pi G\rho_b$ (\S22) at every radius fixes
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_b\equiv\frac{a_{\rm drag}}{g_b}=\nu\!\Big(\frac{g_b}{a_c}\Big)-1\;\longrightarrow\;
\frac{2}{\sqrt3}\Big(\frac{a_c}{g_b}\Big)^{1/2}=\Big(\frac{a_0}{g_b}\Big)^{1/2}\quad(g_b\ll a_c),
\label{eq:gamma-match}
\end{equation}
with $\nu$ the inverse of $\mu$: the geometric-mean form, its coefficient $2/\sqrt3$ set by the isotropic
average of \S22. The zero point is then predicted, not anchored:
\begin{equation}
\omega(r)=\frac{v_{\rm flat}}{2r},\qquad v_{\rm flat}^4=a_0\,G\,M_b,\qquad a_0=\tfrac43\,a_c .
\label{eq:btf-rot}
\end{equation}
At the Sun's orbit the required value is $\Gamma_b=0.51$. The metric part is of order $(v/c)^2$; the
geometric part carries the flat curve, and its source is the cone each baryonic system carries.

\subsection*{49.6\quad The baryonic defect cone}
\sH\ Baryons at any scale generate their own defect cone. The cone's deficit is $\pi$ regardless of
scale; its slant depends on the scale. \sD\ A deficit of $\pi$ fixes the cone's shape: unrolled, the
cone is a half-disc of radius $L$, whose arc $\pi L$ closes into the base circle $2\pi R$, so $R=L/2$, the
half-opening is $30^\circ$, and the section through the axis is an equilateral triangle---the $C_3$
triangle of the second axiom, at every scale. Scale therefore enters only through the slant length $L$.
The defect relation \eqref{eq:defectmass} is the $2{+}1$ reduction with length $L=r$ (I3); with each
system's own slant as its reduction length, a deficit of $\pi$ requires
\begin{equation}
\pi=\frac{8\pi GM}{c^2L_\pi}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad L_\pi=\frac{8GM}{c^2}=4\,r_s ,
\label{eq:Lpi}
\end{equation}
four Schwarzschild radii for any mass: compactness $GM/c^2L_\pi=\tfrac18$, a quarter of a black hole's---the
strength of almost a black hole, without a black hole. For the horizon's cone mass,
$M_{\rm cone}=\MH/4=c^2r/8G$, it returns $L_\pi=r$, the horizon's own cone; for the Sun it is $11.8$ km, and
for the Milky Way's baryons, $6\times10^{10}M_\odot$, $4.7\times10^3$ AU.

A cone of fixed shape supplies one dimensionless coupling, a ratio of lengths, $\Gamma_b=r/\ell$. The
matching \eqref{eq:gamma-match} requires
\begin{equation}
\ell=\sqrt{\frac{GM_b}{a_0}}=\sqrt{L_\pi\cdot\frac{c^2}{8a_0}},\qquad \frac{c^2}{8a_0}=\frac{\pi^2}{8\sqrt3}\,r ,
\label{eq:ell-cone}
\end{equation}
the geometric mean of the cone's slant $L_\pi$ and a fixed horizon length: $7.2\times10^3$ AU for the
Sun and $8.5$ kpc for the Milky Way's baryons, the radius at which the system's own pull equals $a_0$.
Inside it the cone's coupling is weak and the motion Newtonian; beyond it the coupling grows as $r/\ell$
and the curve is flat, with $v_{\rm flat}^4=a_0GM_b$.

\subsection*{49.7\quad The coupling length: gravity in two dimensions}
\sD\ The deep-regime field of an isolated mass, $g=\sqrt{GMa_0}/r$, falls as $1/r$: it is exactly the field of
the same mass under two-dimensional Poisson gravity,
\begin{equation}
\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi\,\frac{G}{L_{\rm red}}\,\Sigma,\qquad g=\frac{2GM}{L_{\rm red}\,r},\qquad L_{\rm red}=2\sqrt{\frac{GM}{a_0}} .
\label{eq:2d-law}
\end{equation}
\sI\ The cone supplies the reduction. The defect relation \eqref{eq:defectmass} is already the $2{+}1$
reduction $G_3=G/L$ (I3); beyond its crossover, each baryonic system's gravity is that of its cone's
two-dimensional section. \sD\ The coupling length then follows in four steps:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep1pt
\item a deficit of $\pi$ fixes the cone's shape and its slant, $L_\pi=8GM/c^2$ \eqref{eq:Lpi};
\item inside the crossover the field is three-dimensional, $GM/x^2$; beyond it, two-dimensional,
$2GM/(L_{\rm red}x)$;
\item the two laws meet at $x_c=L_{\rm red}/2$;
\item the crossover lies at the horizon's acceleration (\S10): $GM/x_c^2=a_0$.
\end{enumerate}
Hence
\begin{equation}
\ell=x_c=\sqrt{\frac{GM}{a_0}},\qquad L_{\rm red}^2=L_\pi\,R_{a_0},\qquad
R_{a_0}=\frac{c^2}{2a_0}=z_H\,r,\qquad z_H=\frac{\pi^2}{2\sqrt3}=2.849 .
\label{eq:Lred}
\end{equation}
$R_{a_0}$ is the radius of the horizon whose surface gravity $c^2/2R$ equals $a_0$, and $z_H$ is the horizon
load of \S25.7. The reduction length is the geometric mean of the system's own $\pi$-cone slant, four
Schwarzschild radii, and the horizon whose surface gravity is $a_0$: the altitude of the right triangle
whose hypotenuse is cut into $L_\pi$ and $R_{a_0}$. The Tully--Fisher law follows with nothing put in,
\begin{equation}
v_{\rm flat}^2=\frac{2GM_b}{L_{\rm red}}=\sqrt{GM_ba_0},\qquad v_{\rm flat}^4=a_0GM_b,
\end{equation}
and the frame field takes the same form: $\omega=v_{\rm flat}/2r$ and $a_{\rm drag}=2\omega v=2GM_b/(L_{\rm red}r)$.
For the Sun $L_{\rm red}=2.15\times10^{15}$ m ($\ell=7{,}199$ AU); for the Milky Way's baryons,
$6\times10^{10}M_\odot$, $L_{\rm red}=5.3\times10^{20}$ m ($\ell=8.55$ kpc).

\subsection*{49.8\quad Tests}
\sP\ \emph{The Tully--Fisher zero point.} Gas-rich galaxies, whose baryonic masses are least affected by
stellar-population modelling, give $a_0=(1.24\pm0.14)\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ with the slope fixed at
four \cite{McGaugh2011}; $a_0=1.148\times10^{-10}$ lies $0.7\sigma$ below.

\sP\ \emph{Wide binaries.} The law acts where the rotation's modulation is bound to the system
(\S49.11). A star's escape speed is far below the motions of the Galaxy's medium, so its capture fraction
is negligible ($7\times10^{-9}$ for the Sun, \S47.2): wide binaries carry no bound medium and are Newtonian
at all separations. This agrees with the Newtonian Gaia DR3 analysis,
$\alpha_{\rm grav}=-0.021^{+0.065}_{-0.045}$ \cite{Banik2024}; a confirmed boost such as the
$22^{+10}_{-6}\%$ reported beyond $3000$ AU \cite{Chae2024,Hernandez2024} would falsify it. Two alternatives
were examined and are excluded: a crossover set by each system's own pull gives boosts of $1.33$ and $1.69$
at $10$ and $20$ kAU, above both analyses; a crossover set by the total field gives a $10\%$ boost and,
in the Solar System, the quadrupole of \S49.11, excluded at $16\sigma$.

\subsection*{49.9\quad The magnetic field and the chiral current}
\sD\ The anomaly coupling $\mathcal L\supset-(\kappa\alpha/\pi)\,\theta\,\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B$ (\S52.1) adds the
current of axion electrodynamics \cite{Wilczek1987} to Amp\`ere's law,
\begin{equation}
\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j+\frac1{c^2}\frac{\partial\mathbf E}{\partial t}-\frac{\kappa\alpha}{\pi}\,\frac{\dot\theta}{c}\,\mathbf B+\cdots,
\end{equation}
so the rotating phase drives a current along $\mathbf B$. In a conductor it acts as an $\alpha$-effect,
unstable below the wavenumber $k_\theta=(\kappa\alpha/\pi)\,\dot\theta/c$ and fastest at $k_\theta/2$. For the
global rotation, $\dot\theta=\Omega_0=0.827H$, the fastest-growing wavelength is $4\pi^2c/(0.827\,\kappa\alpha H)$,
$1{,}090$ Hubble radii for $\kappa=6$ at every epoch; for the Galaxy's rotation at the Sun,
$\Omega=v/r=9.2\times10^{-16}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, it is $2.1$ Hubble radii. The anomaly current cannot grow a
field inside the horizon. The observed fields are set by ordinary dynamo saturation, at equipartition
with the turbulent motions,
\begin{equation}
B_{\rm eq}=\sqrt{\mu_0\rho}\;v_t\,\sqrt{D/D_{\rm cr}-1}\;\longrightarrow\;\sqrt{\mu_0\rho}\;v_t ,
\end{equation}
$4.6\ \mu$G for $\rho=1.67\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and $v_t=10$ km s$^{-1}$, the Milky Way's value
(the rotation speed in place of $v_t$ would give $107\ \mu$G). In the flat regime the local phase frequency
equals the galaxy's angular velocity, $\Omega=v_{\rm flat}/r$ (\S49.2), which also drives the
differential-rotation dynamo: the field and the frame dragging are driven by the same local $\Omega$.

\sD\ In relativistic chiral matter the rotation carries an axial current, $\mathbf j_A=\mu^2\boldsymbol\omega/2\pi^2$
for each Dirac species ($\hbar=c=1$, $T=0$) \cite{Vilenkin1979,SonSurowka2009}, so
$\mathbf j_A=\sum_fN_c\,\mu_f^2\,\boldsymbol\omega/2\pi^2$; the anomaly coefficient of the
$\theta\,\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B$ coupling over three generations is $\sum_fN_cQ_f^2=3+4+1=8$. It acts where the
fermions are relativistic: the early universe and neutron-star interiors.

\subsection*{49.10\quad The Solar-System quadrupole}
\sP\ In the Galactic field the Sun's potential can acquire the quadrupole
\begin{equation}
\Phi=-\frac{GM_\odot}{r}-\frac{Q_2}{2}\,x^ix^j\Big(e_ie_j-\frac13\delta_{ij}\Big),
\end{equation}
with $\hat e$ toward the Galactic centre \cite{Hees2014}. In the complete equation (\S46--47) the
Sun carries no bound condensate, for two independent reasons: the Solar System lies far inside the
condensate's Jeans length ($40$--$290$ pc), and the Sun, formed inside the Galaxy, cannot capture a
field streaming past at $\sim165$ km\,s$^{-1}$ with an escape velocity of $0.5$ km\,s$^{-1}$ at its
MOND radius (capture fraction $7\times10^{-9}$, \S47.2). The only quadrupole is the Galactic tide,
\begin{equation}
|Q_2|\sim\frac{V_0^2}{R_0^2}=8.6\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}},
\end{equation}
against the Cassini range data reduced with DE440, $(1.6\pm1.8)\times10^{-27}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}$
\cite{DE440Q2} ($-0.9\sigma$), and the earlier $(3\pm3)\times10^{-27}$ \cite{Hees2014}.

\emph{Why the condensate must be the source.} With the baryons as the only source---the
quasi-static law of \S22---the quadrupole is set by the Sun's phantom density
$\rho_{\rm ph}=\nabla\cdot[(\nu-1)\nabla\Phi_N]/4\pi G$ in the Galactic field, and since
$P_2(\cos\theta)/r^3$ is harmonic,
\begin{equation}
Q_2=\frac{3}{4\pi}\int(\nu-1)\,\mathbf g_N\cdot\nabla\Big(\frac{P_2(\cos\theta)}{r^3}\Big)\,d^3y .
\label{eq:Q2}
\end{equation}
Evaluated with $\nu-1=(1-\mu)/\mu$ free of cancellation and the saddle point resolved (the simple
and standard functions give $2.80\times10^{-26}$ and $1.98\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}$ at
$a_0=1.2\times10^{-10}$, inside the published range $2.1\times10^{-27}$--$4.1\times10^{-26}$
\cite{BlanchetNovak2011,HeesPPN}), the threshold family gives $2.8\times10^{-26}$ at
$g_e=1.9\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ and $3.1\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}$ at
$g_e=V_0^2/R_0$, twenty times what Cassini allows. A boost that belongs to the baryons' field,
rather than to matter bound to each system, cannot hold in the Solar System; every modified-gravity
law that fits rotation curves meets the same bound \cite{Desmond2024}. That is the incompleteness
that \S46 repairs.

\subsection*{49.11\quad Where the law acts}
\sD\ \emph{The two-dimensional law is derived.} The deep limit of the law of \S22, $\mu\to\tfrac34x$, gives
$g=\sqrt{a_0g_N}$ and therefore the $1/r$ field \eqref{eq:2d-law}. Reading it as the cone's section
(\S49.7) gives $L_{\rm red}$ its geometric meaning; the predictions need nothing more.

\sD\ \emph{The law acts where the medium is bound.} The extra gravity of a system is carried by the
rotation's modulation bound to it (\S23.1): its polarization around the baryons has the permittivity
$\mu$ of \S46.6, a theorem that uses the threshold response and no particle mass. Whether the medium is
bound is the capture condition of \S47.2, $\Gamma=\mathrm{erf}(X)-(2X/\sqrt\pi)e^{-X^2}$ with
$X=v_{\rm esc}/\sqrt2\sigma$, set by the motions of the medium and not by a mass. Galaxies and clusters,
formed inside the cosmic medium, bind it ($\Gamma\to1$); a star does not ($\Gamma=7\times10^{-9}$ for the
Sun). Every level of structure drags the frame inside it through its own bound medium: the Galaxy's
medium sets the Sun's orbit, and no medium of the Sun's own alters the planets.

\sP\ \emph{The test that decides it.} Applied instead to the total field of the Galaxy and the Sun, every
form of the law of \S22 gives the Solar System an external-field quadrupole
\begin{equation}
Q_2=(3.0\text{--}3.2)\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}
\end{equation}
(threshold orders $1$--$3$ and the dynamic exponent; the method of Appendix~\ref{app:numbers}), twenty times
the Cassini value $(1.6\pm1.8)\times10^{-27}$ \cite{DE440Q2}: excluded at $16\sigma$. With the medium unbound
around the Sun, $Q_2$ is the Galactic tide alone, $9\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}$, at $-0.9\sigma$ (variance row~21).

\sD\ \emph{Extended systems.} For a bound system the field equation of \S22 is local and conservative,
and its isolated-mass solution is \eqref{eq:2d-law}; extended and non-spherical systems obey it directly.

\subsection*{49.12\quad Birefringence}
\sD\ Held at its rate by \eqref{eq:rot-constraint}, the phase advances uniformly, $\theta=\Omega_0t$, and through the
anomaly coupling $-(\kappa\alpha/\pi)\,\theta\,\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B$ (\S52.1) it rotates the plane of polarization of light
by half the coupling times the phase advanced in flight. For the CMB,
\begin{equation}
\beta=\frac{\kappa\alpha}{2\pi}\,\Omega_0\,(t_0-t_{\rm rec})=0.052^\circ\,\kappa ,
\end{equation}
with $\Omega_0(t_0-t_{\rm rec})=0.781$ rad, and its sign is the sense of the rotation, the handedness of the
two-C/S structure. \sD\ The coupling is the anomaly of the fermions whose masses the phase enters, those on the cone
(\S42.3, \S42.7). Removing the phase from their mass terms is a chiral rotation of unit weight, whose anomaly
gives $\kappa=\sum_fN_cQ_f^2$ over them \cite{Wilczek1987}: $1+1+1=3$ for the charged leptons and
$3(\tfrac49+\tfrac19+\tfrac49)=3$ for $(c,b,t)$, so $\kappa=6$ and
\begin{equation}
\beta=0.314^\circ ,
\end{equation}
$0.6\sigma$ above the joint ACT and Planck value $0.277^\circ\pm0.057^\circ$ \cite{Eskilt2026}; with the charged leptons
alone, $\kappa=3$ and $\beta=0.156^\circ$, $2.1\sigma$ below. This replaces, in the rotation reading, the frozen phase of
\S52.1, whose bound $|\beta|<10^{-3}{}^\circ$ left a confirmed cosmological angle a $4.9\sigma$ problem. With the rate set by the
spin and the present at the resonance the angle takes a closed form, $\beta=(2\alpha/\pi)\,\mathrm{artanh}(3\sqrt3/2\pi)=0.3137^\circ$
(\S36.13). With the first-starlight hypothesis the same weighted phase dates cosmic dawn: $\alpha$ cancels, and
$H_\infty t_\star=(2\pi/\kappa)\,\beta$ (\S48.8).

\subsection*{49.13\quad One rotation, from galaxies to the oldest light}
\sD\ The acceleration scale and the birefringence angle are set by the same rotation. The critical
acceleration is the photon's acceleration over one turn of the $C_3$ eigenmode, $a_c=c/\tau_1$ with
$\tau_1=2\pi/\Omega_0$, and $a_0=\tfrac43a_c$, so
\begin{equation}
\Omega_0=\frac{2\pi a_c}{c}=\frac{3\pi}{2}\,\frac{a_0}{c} .
\end{equation}
(The eigenmode is the current horizon's, and its period equals the rotation's turn at the present, the resonance; the relation holds at the
present, while $a_0$ itself tracks the horizon at other epochs, \S36.15.) The angle is the phase advanced between last scattering and today, weighted by the anomaly,
$\beta=\kappa\alpha\,\Omega_0(t_0-t_{\rm rec})/2\pi$ (\S49.12). Eliminating the rotation, and dropping
$t_{\rm rec}/t_0=3\times10^{-5}$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\beta=\frac{3\kappa\alpha}{4}\,\frac{a_0t_0}{c}=\frac{9\alpha}{2}\,\frac{a_0t_0}{c}}\qquad(\kappa=6).
\label{eq:beta-a0}
\end{equation}
Each factor is measured by a different science: $\beta$ in the polarization of the CMB, $\alpha$ in
atomic physics, $a_0$ in galaxy rotation curves, $t_0$ in the cosmic age. With the framework's own
$a_0=1.148\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ and $t_0=13.79$ Gyr the relation gives $0.314^\circ$, as \S49.12
does by the other route; with the radial acceleration relation's $a_0=(1.20\pm0.24)\times10^{-10}\
\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ \cite{McGaugh2016} and $t_0=13.80$ Gyr it gives $0.33^\circ\pm0.07^\circ$. The measured
angle is $0.277^\circ\pm0.057^\circ$ \cite{Eskilt2026}, $0.6\sigma$ from both: galaxy rotation curves
predict the rotation of the CMB's polarization. Milgrom's coincidence $2\pi a_0\approx cH_0$
\cite{Milgrom1983} ties $a_0$ to the expansion; equation~\eqref{eq:beta-a0} ties it, through $\alpha$, to the
polarization of the oldest light, and to our knowledge no relation between the MOND acceleration and
cosmic birefringence has been proposed before. \sP\ The relation tests the whole chain at once: the rate
fixed by the $C_3$ eigenvalue, the rotation's being internal and held at its rate, and the anomaly weight
$\kappa=6$ of the cone fermions. With $a_0$ known to $5\%$ (now $20\%$) and $\beta$ to $0.02^\circ$, a
ratio $\beta/\alpha$ more than $5\sigma$ from $\tfrac92a_0t_0/c$ excludes it (register row 62).

\section{The closure law and the scale ladder}\label{sec:closure}

\subsection*{50.1\quad The law}
\sD\ Energy is defect. By the defect map~\eqref{eq:defectmass} every energy $E$ inside the horizon carries the deficit
\begin{equation}
\delta(E)=\frac{8\pi GE}{c^4r}=4\pi\,\frac{r_s(E)}{r},\qquad r_s=\frac{2GE}{c^4},
\label{eq:defect-rs}
\end{equation}
$4\pi$ times the ratio of its Schwarzschild radius to the horizon radius. \sS\ On a closed surface the total
curvature is topological, $\sum_i\delta_i+\int K\,dA=2\pi\chi$ (Gauss--Bonnet). In $2{+}1$ gravity, where all
curvature sits in point defects, a closed universe therefore has total deficit exactly $4\pi$ and a fixed total
mass \cite{DJtH1984,Brill2003}. \sD\ The horizon is the $2{+}1$ surface on which, by the reduction of~\eqref{eq:defectmass},
every interior energy appears as a defect, and it is closed, $\chi=2$. Its defects sum to $4\pi$:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\sum_i r_{s,i}=\frac{\chi}{2}\,r,\qquad \Omega_{\rm tot}=\frac{\chi}{2}=1 .}
\label{eq:closure}
\end{equation}
The Schwarzschild radii of everything inside the horizon add up to the horizon, and the total energy is the
Hubble mass, $\sum_iE_i=\MH c^2=c^4r/2G$. Planck gives $\Omega_{\rm tot}=1.0007\pm0.0019$ \cite{Planck2018},
$0.4\sigma$. \sD\ Energy conservation inside the horizon is then topological: the total cannot change without
changing $\chi$, and it grows only as $r$ grows. Brill noted that the $2{+}1$ closure is analogous to
$GM/c^2R\sim1$ for a closed universe \cite{Brill2003}; with the defect map the analogy becomes the exact
equality~\eqref{eq:closure}, and flatness becomes the Euler characteristic of the horizon. \sS\ The smallest
closed universe of $2{+}1$ gravity has three particles on two triangular faces glued at their edges, three being
the fewest that define an inertial frame \cite{Brill2003}: the $C_3$ cycle is the minimal closed horizon.

\subsection*{50.2\quad The ladder}
\sD\ By Gauss--Bonnet a defect $\delta$ is the solid angle swept by the normals around it, and the information of
a defect is its share of the cells, so energy, defect, horizon area and information are one fraction:
\begin{equation}
\frac{E}{\MH c^2}=\frac{\delta}{4\pi}=\frac{A}{4\pi r^2}=\frac{I}{N_{\rm full}},\qquad N_{\rm full}=\frac{4\pi r^2}{\ell_P^2}=2N .
\label{eq:shares}
\end{equation}
The elementary symmetric cut is the $\mathbb Z_2$ of the deficit $\pi$. The quotient $S^2/\mathbb Z_k$ has orbifold
Euler characteristic $2/k$, so by~\eqref{eq:closure} it carries $\MH c^2/k$, and the iterated $\mathbb Z_2$ cut is the
ladder
\begin{equation}
E_n=\frac{\MH c^2}{2^n},\qquad \delta_n=\frac{4\pi}{2^n},\qquad I_n=\frac{N_{\rm full}}{2^n}.
\label{eq:ladder}
\end{equation}
Its rungs are geometric. At $n=0$ the deficit is $4\pi$, the closed horizon, and the energy the Hubble mass,
$5.205\times10^{88}$ eV. At $n=1$ it is $2\pi$, the critical mass of $2{+}1$ gravity beyond which a universe must
close. At $n=2$ it is $\pi$, the total of the $C_3$ triple, $M_{\rm cone}=\MH/4$ (I1). One generator, $\pi/3$, sits at
$n=\log_212=3.585$, so the triadic rungs of $C_3$ interleave the dyadic ones. The ladder ends at one cell, the
